Quantifying the cost of measles outbreak in the U.S. and how costs scale with outbreak size

Authors: Salin Sriudomporn, Bryan Patenaude
Original abstract (English)
In 2025, the United States experienced the largest number of measles cases since its elimination in 2000. These outbreaks require extensive public health responses to mitigate, consume substantial hospital resources, and impose an economic and financial burden on state and federal budgets. We conducted a literature review using PubMed as well as official CDC and state health department reports to identify studies published between January 1, 2000, and October 7, 2025, across all 50 states. We also examined how costs scale with outbreak size. A total of 120 articles were screened, 33 underwent full text review, and data were extracted from 19 articles, yielding outbreak relevant costs in 18 different states. Eight additional reports in the gray literature were identified. Outbreak size ranged from 1 to 802 cases. Average outbreak cost per case was estimated at $43,203.65, while the average cost per contact was $443.46. Average cost per case varied from $33,415.75 from the medical provider perspective to $58,591.50 when including public health response costs. Fixed costs of imitating public health responses were estimated at $297,746.94 (95% CI: $4537.86 - $590,956.02), with an incremental cost of $16,299.64 (95% CI: $15,301.96 - $17,297.32) per each additional case. The elasticity of the total costs with respect to outbreak size was estimated at 0.86 (95% CI: 0.65-1.07) Measles outbreaks in the U.S. continue to re-emerge and impose significant financial and public health burden. These outbreaks are largely preventable through inexpensive and highly effective measles vaccination paired with system-level preparedness for effective containment. Since fixed response costs for any outbreak can be costly, our estimates provide improved data on how costs of measles outbreaks scale with outbreak size by isolating incremental costs associated incident new cases, as well as incident contacts, for use alongside budgetary planning and modeled risk assessments.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
In 2025, the United States experienced the largest number of measles cases since its elimination in 2000.
Evidence base and method
We conducted a literature review using PubMed as well as official CDC and state health department reports to identify studies published between January 1, 2000, and October 7, 2025, across all 50 states. A total of 120 articles were screened, 33 underwent full text review, and data were extracted from 19 articles, yielding outbreak relevant costs in 18 different states. We also examined how costs scale with outbreak size.
Consensus and conclusions
Average outbreak cost per case was estimated at $43,203.65, while the average cost per contact was $443.46. The elasticity of the total costs with respect to outbreak size was estimated at 0.86 (95% CI: 0.65-1.07) Measles outbreaks in the U.S.
Controversies and gaps
Average cost per case varied from $33,415.75 from the medical provider perspective to $58,591.50 when including public health response costs. Fixed costs of imitating public health responses were estimated at $297,746.94 (95% CI: $4537.86 - $590,956.02), with an incremental cost of $16,299.64 (95% CI: $15,301.96 - $17,297.32) per each additional case.
Research and practice implications
Measles outbreaks continue to re-emerge and impose significant financial and public health burden. These outbreaks are largely preventable through inexpensive and highly effective measles vaccination paired with system-level preparedness for effective containment.

疫苗接种 微针:机械基础、材料创新、临床转化和全球健康实施

Microneedles for Vaccination: Mechanistic Foundations, Materials Innovation, Clinical Translation, and Global Health Implementation
作者： Hiep X. Nguyen, Mai Phuong Ho
摘要中文翻译疫苗接种 属于最重要的医学干预措施之一,但冷链依赖、锐器危险、针头恐惧症和对训练有素的疫苗接种员的依赖限制了 疫苗 可预防的 死亡率 的覆盖范围。微针贴片通过穿透角质层的突出物将抗原沉积到富含抗原呈递细胞的表皮和上层真皮中,但不会到达真皮伤害感受器。这种靶向产生的免疫原性与抗原质量的一小部分相匹配或超过肌内注射,流感、脊髓灰质炎和 SARS-CoV-2 抗原的剂量节省高达六倍。固态制剂将这种免疫学优势转化为物流优势,因为聚合物基质在环境温度下可保持效力长达两年,从而消除了消耗大量运输成本的制冷基础设施。六种微针类型已达到临床前或临床成熟,其中可溶性微针贴片最为先进。两项试验提供了临床证据:一项 I 期流感研究显示出非劣效的抗体反应和成功的自我应用,以及一项在冈比亚进行的 I/II 期 麻疹-风疹试验,婴儿中 麻疹 达到 93%,风疹血清转化率为 100%,没有相关的严重不良事件。目前的工程进展包括自动打印机,可生产耐热 mRNA-脂质纳米颗粒贴片,在环境温度下可保持生物活性六个月,第一个皮内自放大 RNA 贴片,以及量子点体内免疫记录。无菌保证、剂量均匀性、固体基质内的核酸完整性和分散的监管指导仍然是具有挑战性的障碍,同时临床管道集中于少数抗原。本次审查整合了该领域的机械、材料、制造、临床、监管和全球健康维度,以指导翻译和公平部署。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本次审查的重点是微针在 疫苗接种 中的使用,特别是在 麻疹病毒 的背景下。核心问题是,考虑到微针的免疫原性、后勤优势和临床转化潜力,微针是否可以作为 麻疹 疫苗接种 传统注射方法的有效替代品。
证据基础与方法
该综述对微针技术的机械、材料、制造、临床、监管和全球健康维度进行了全面分析。它包括两项临床试验:一项 I 期流感研究和一项在冈比亚进行的 I/II 期 麻疹 风疹试验。该综述综合了这些试验以及其他临床前和临床研究的结果,以评估微针用于 麻疹 疫苗接种 的潜力。
共识与结论
微针贴片的免疫原性与肌肉注射相当或更优,且在针对麻疹抗原时观察到剂量节约效益。在冈比亚进行的麻疹–风疹试验中,婴儿的麻疹血清阳转率为93%,风疹血清阳转率为100%,且未出现严重不良事件,支持微针技术在麻疹疫苗接种中的临床潜力。
争议与缺口
该综述强调了固体基质中的无菌保证、剂量均匀性和核酸完整性等挑战。此外,临床管道集中在有限数量的抗原上,监管指导仍然支离破碎,导致在麻疹 疫苗接种中更广泛应用微针的证据方面存在差距。
科研与实践启示
该审查表明需要对 麻疹 的微针技术进行进一步研究,包括无菌性、剂量均匀性和监管框架的改进。它还强调了扩大临床管道以包括更多 麻疹 相关抗原并确保在全球卫生环境中公平部署该技术的重要性。

Microneedles for Vaccination: Mechanistic Foundations, Materials Innovation, Clinical Translation, and Global Health Implementation

Authors: Hiep X. Nguyen, Mai Phuong Ho
Original abstract (English)
Vaccination ranks among the most consequential interventions in medicine, yet cold-chain dependence, sharps hazards, needle phobia, and reliance on trained vaccinators limit coverage where vaccine-preventable mortality is highest. Microneedle patches deposit antigen into the antigen-presenting-cell-rich epidermis and upper dermis through projections that penetrate the stratum corneum without reaching dermal nociceptors. Such targeting yields immunogenicity matching or exceeding intramuscular injection at a fraction of the antigen mass, with dose-sparing up to six-fold recorded for influenza, polio, and SARS-CoV-2 antigens. Solid-state formulation converts that immunological advantage into a logistical one, since polymeric matrices preserve potency for as long as two years at ambient temperature, removing the refrigeration infrastructure that consumes significant delivery cost. Six microneedle types have reached preclinical or clinical maturity, with dissolving microneedle patches the most advanced. Two trials provide the clinical evidence: a Phase I influenza study showing non-inferior antibody responses and successful self-application, and a Phase I/II measles–rubella trial in The Gambia reaching 93% measles and 100% rubella seroconversion in infants without related serious adverse events. Engineering advances currently include an automated printer producing thermostable mRNA–lipid nanoparticle patches that retain bioactivity for six months at ambient temperature, the first intradermal self-amplifying RNA patch, and quantum-dot on-body immunization records. Sterility assurance, dose uniformity, nucleic acid integrity within solid matrices, and fragmented regulatory guidance remain the challenging obstacles, alongside a clinical pipeline concentrated on few antigens. This review integrates the mechanistic, materials, manufacturing, clinical, regulatory, and global health dimensions of the field to guide translation and equitable deployment.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on the use of microneedles for vaccination, particularly in the context of measles virus. The central question is whether microneedles can serve as an effective and efficient alternative to traditional injection methods for measles vaccination, considering their immunogenicity, logistical advantages, and clinical translation potential.
Evidence base and method
The review is a comprehensive analysis of the mechanistic, materials, manufacturing, clinical, regulatory, and global health dimensions of microneedle technology. It includes two clinical trials: a Phase I influenza study and a Phase I/II measles–rubella trial in The Gambia. The review integrates findings from these trials and other preclinical and clinical studies to evaluate the potential of microneedles for measles vaccination.
Consensus and conclusions
Microneedle patches demonstrate immunogenicity matching or exceeding intramuscular injection, with dose-sparing benefits observed for measles antigens. The measles–rubella trial in The Gambia showed 93% measles and 100% rubella seroconversion in infants without serious adverse events, supporting the clinical potential of microneedles for measles vaccination.
Controversies and gaps
The review highlights challenges such as sterility assurance, dose uniformity, and nucleic acid integrity within solid matrices. Additionally, the clinical pipeline is concentrated on a limited number of antigens, and regulatory guidance remains fragmented, creating gaps in the evidence for broader application of microneedles in measles vaccination.
Research and practice implications
The review suggests the need for further research on microneedle technology for measles, including improvements in sterility, dose uniformity, and regulatory frameworks. It also emphasizes the importance of expanding the clinical pipeline to include more measles-related antigens and ensuring equitable deployment of this technology in global health settings.

dupilumab 治疗期间的 MMR 和水痘免疫。

MMR and varicella immunization during dupilumab therapy.
作者： Hannah Seay, Marissa Perman, James Treat, Fahad Ahmed, Terri Brown-Whitehorn, Jonathan Spergel, Jennifer Heimall
摘要中文翻译背景:美国食品药品监督管理局(FDA)建议在使用度普利单抗(dupilumab)治疗期间,因缺乏安全性和有效性数据,推迟接种活疫苗疫苗。了解度普利单抗治疗期间接种麻疹、腮腺炎、风疹(MMR)和水痘疫苗的安全性,对促进适当的疫苗接种具有重要意义。目的:本研究旨在评估在使用度普利单抗期间接种MMR和水痘疫苗的安全性,以及度普利单抗对儿童早期MMR和水痘疫苗接种率的影响。方法:本研究为单中心回顾性分析,纳入年龄小于9岁且正在使用度普利单抗且拥有疫苗接种记录的儿童。主要结局指标包括疫苗相关感染、疫苗相关不良事件及疫苗接种率。结果:在19名于度普利单抗治疗期间接种MMR和水痘疫苗的患者中,无人出现严重疫苗相关感染或严重疫苗相关不良事件。1名患者报告发热和注射部位反应,5名患者报告特应性皮炎(AD)加重。总计21剂水痘加强针(9.7%)和25剂MMR加强针(11.5%)因患者正在使用度普利单抗而延迟接种。在比较度普利单抗治疗前后的接种年龄时,MMR加强针和水痘加强针的接种年龄均出现显著延迟。结论:MMR和水痘疫苗接种在使用度普利单抗的儿童中可能是安全的。为接种活疫苗而暂停度普利单抗治疗可能导致潜在特应性皮炎加重。此外,度普利单抗在儿童早期的使用可能导致MMR和水痘疫苗接种率降低和/或接种延迟。研究结果支持近期建议:在使用度普利单抗期间不应推迟疫苗接种。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述调查了儿童在 dupilumab 治疗期间 麻疹、腮腺炎、风疹 (MMR) 和水痘免疫接种的安全性,并检查了 dupilumab 对这些疾病的 疫苗接种 率的影响。
证据基础与方法
对正在服用 dupilumab 并有可用 疫苗接种 记录的 9 岁以下儿童进行了单中心回顾性审查。主要结局指标包括 疫苗 相关感染、疫苗 相关不良事件以及免疫接种率。
共识与结论
研究发现,19名患者中无人出现严重疫苗相关不良事件或感染。1名患者报告发热和注射部位反应,5名患者报告特应性皮炎加重。此外,在度普利单抗治疗期间,MMR和水痘疫苗接种存在显著延迟。研究结果支持近期建议:在使用度普利单抗期间不应推迟疫苗接种。
争议与缺口
该评论指出,暂停 Dupilumab 治疗活 疫苗 可能会导致潜在的特应性皮炎发作。然而,证据并未提供这些 疫苗 对接受 dupilumab 治疗的儿童的长期安全性或有效性的详细信息。
科研与实践启示
该研究强调了了解接受 dupilumab 治疗的儿童 MMR 和水痘免疫接种的安全性的重要性。它表明在dupilumab 治疗期间不应延迟疫苗接种,但需要进一步的研究来证实这些发现并解决潜在的长期影响。

MMR and varicella immunization during dupilumab therapy.

Authors: Hannah Seay, Marissa Perman, James Treat, Fahad Ahmed, Terri Brown-Whitehorn, Jonathan Spergel, Jennifer Heimall
Original abstract (English)
Background: The US Food and Drug Administration recommends deferring administration of live vaccines during dupilumab treatment owing to lack of data on safety and efficacy. Understanding the safety profile of measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) and varicella administration in children taking dupilumab is important to promote appropriate vaccination. Objective: Our aim was to determine the safety of MMR and varicella immunizations during treatment with dupilumab and the impact of dupilumab on MMR and varicella vaccination rates in early childhood. Methods: We performed a single-center retrospective review of children younger than 9 years who were taking dupilumab and had available vaccination records. The primary outcome measures were vaccine-associated infections, vaccine-associated adverse events, and rate of immunization. Results: Of the 19 patients who received MMR and varicella immunization while taking dupilumab, none experienced severe vaccine-associated events or vaccine-associated infections. One patient reported fever and injection site reaction, and 5 reported a flare of atopic dermatitis (AD). In all, administration of 21 varicella boosters (9.7%) and 25 MMR boosters (11.5%) were delayed because the children were taking dupilumab. There was a significant delay in the age of administration of MMR boosters and varicella boosters when before- and after-dupilumab therapy rates were compared. Conclusion: MMR and varicella immunization may be safe to administer to children undergoing dupilumab therapy. Pausing dupilumab therapy for administration of live vaccines may lead to flare of underlying AD. Further, dupilumab treatment in early childhood may result in decreased and/or delayed vaccination rates for MMR and varicella. Our findings support the recent recommendation that immunizations not be delayed while patients are receiving dupilumab.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review investigates the safety of measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) and varicella immunizations during dupilumab therapy in children and examines the impact of dupilumab on vaccination rates for these diseases.
Evidence base and method
A single-center retrospective review of children younger than 9 years who were taking dupilumab and had available vaccination records was conducted. The primary outcome measures included vaccine-associated infections, vaccine-associated adverse events, and the rate of immunization.
Consensus and conclusions
The study found that none of the 19 patients experienced severe vaccine-associated events or infections. One patient reported fever and injection site reaction, and 5 reported a flare of atopic dermatitis. Additionally, there was a significant delay in the administration of MMR and varicella vaccines during dupilumab therapy. The findings support the recent recommendation that immunizations should not be delayed while patients are receiving dupilumab.
Controversies and gaps
The review notes that pausing dupilumab therapy for live vaccines may lead to a flare of underlying atopic dermatitis. However, the evidence does not provide detailed information on the long-term safety or efficacy of these vaccines in children on dupilumab therapy.
Research and practice implications
The study highlights the importance of understanding the safety profile of MMR and varicella immunizations in children undergoing dupilumab therapy. It suggests that vaccination should not be delayed during dupilumab treatment, but further research is needed to confirm these findings and address potential long-term effects.

开发新型冻干减毒活 麻疹-风疹 (MR) 疫苗 制剂,专为非洲低成本生产而设计

Development of a New Lyophilized Live-Attenuated Measles-Rubella (MR) Vaccine Formulation Designed for Lower Cost Production in Africa

吉尔吉斯斯坦 麻疹 暴发 应对措施的评估 - 世界卫生组织 (WHO)

Assessment of the measles outbreak response in Kyrgyzstan - World Health Organization (WHO)
新闻简报世界卫生组织 (WHO) 于 2026 年 8 月 17 日至 18 日在比什凯克对吉尔吉斯斯坦的 麻疹 暴发 应对措施进行了评估。麻疹 麻疹 暴发 于 2023 年初开始,并蔓延到该国所有地区。对此,吉尔吉斯斯坦政府在世界卫生组织的技术支持下,组织了四轮针对9-84月龄儿童的大规模补充免疫活动。前两轮疫苗接种的覆盖率分别达到85%和79%,第三轮和第四轮达到88%和74%的覆盖率。该评估旨在评估国家卫生系统的行动,找出优势和差距,并制定行动计划以加强准备和应对。它还将 麻疹 准备工作纳入更广泛的健康安全规划中。该活动由欧盟共同资助并得到全球疫苗免疫联盟的支持。这些发现将为未来预防和应对 麻疹 暴发 和其他 疫苗 可预防疾病威胁的策略提供信息。
查看新闻五要素
时间
评估于 2026 年 8 月 17 日至 18 日进行,麻疹 暴发 于 2023 年初开始,活动于 2024 年 10 月持续进行。
地点与对象
该评估在吉尔吉斯斯坦比什凯克进行,全国各地的国家公共卫生组织和国际合作伙伴都有参与。
事件
世卫组织与吉尔吉斯斯坦卫生部和红十字会与红新月会国际联合会合作,对吉尔吉斯斯坦的 麻疹 暴发 应对措施进行了评估。
规模、影响与风险
吉尔吉斯斯坦仍然是世卫组织欧洲区域报告 麻疹 病例数最多的 10 个国家之一。组织补充免疫活动四轮,覆盖率分别达到85%、79%、88%、74%。
应对、状态与不确定性
该评估确定了立即和长期的纠正行动,制定了行动计划,并将 麻疹 准备工作纳入更广泛的卫生安全规划中。第四轮免疫接种仍在进行中。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Assessment of the measles outbreak response in Kyrgyzstan - World Health Organization (WHO)

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) conducted an assessment of Kyrgyzstan's measles outbreak response in Bishkek on 17–18 August 2026. The measles outbreak began in early 2023 and spread across all regions of the country. In response, the Kyrgyz government, with WHO's technical support, organized four rounds of large-scale supplementary immunization activities targeting children aged 9–84 months. The first two rounds achieved vaccination coverage of 85% and 79%, respectively, while the third and fourth rounds reached 88% and 74% coverage. The assessment aimed to evaluate the national health system's actions, identify strengths and gaps, and develop an action plan to strengthen preparedness and response. It also integrated measles preparedness into broader health security planning. The event was co-funded by the European Union and supported by Gavi. The findings will inform future strategies to prevent and respond to measles outbreaks and other vaccine-preventable disease threats.
Original source text (English)

Videos Photo stories About us Initiatives » An introduction to WHO in the European Region Second European Programme of Work Home/ Events/ Assessment of the measles outbreak response in Kyrgyzstan Previous WHO © Credits WHO © Credits WHO © Credits WHO © Credits WHO © Credits Next 1 / 2 Assessment of the measles outbreak response in Kyrgyzstan 17 – 18 August 2026 Bishkek, Kyrgyzstan Reading time: 1 min (375 words) Event highlights On 17–18 August, in Bishkek, Kyrgyzstan, WHO, in collaboration with the Ministry of Health of Kyrgyzstan and with the support of the International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies, conducted an assessment of the country’s response to the recent measles outbreak. Kyrgyzstan remains among the 10 countries in the WHO European Region with the highest number of reported measles cases, following an outbreak that began in early 2023 and rapidly spread across all regions of the country. In response, the Kyrgyz government, with WHO’s technical support, organized several large-scale supplementary immunization activities. The first 2 rounds, conducted in 2023 and 2024, targeted children aged 9–84 months in 7 regions, achieving vaccination coverage of 85% and 79%, respectively. A third round in 2024, funded by the government and covering Jalal-Abad region, achieved 88% coverage. A fourth round, supported by Gavi and launched in October 2024, is still ongoing, with vaccination coverage reaching 74% by 11 December 2024. Against this backdrop, the assessment aimed to analyse the actions taken by the national health system to respond to the measles outbreak. It identified immediate and longer-term corrective actions to strengthen preparedness and response; developed an action plan to address key gaps and challenges, and make recommendations for strengthening the measles response; and integrated measles preparedness into wider health security planning. The assessment focused on the following key areas and functions: national planning, response coordination and epidemiological surveillance, including the identification, investigation and monitoring of measles cases; risk communication, including community engagement and communication with the public; and vaccination, including vaccine procurement, supply and delivery. Around 40 representatives of national public health organizations from Bishkek and various regions of the country, as well as international health partners, participated in the assessment. Through document reviews, presentations and facilitated group discussions, participants assessed the actions taken during the outbreak, identified strengths and gaps, and drew lessons to inform future preparedness and response. The assessment culminated in a set of priority recommendations and a practical action plan aimed at addressing identified gaps and strengthening Kyrgyzstan’s capacity to prevent, detect and respond effectively to measles outbreaks and other vaccine-preventable disease threats. This event was co-funded by the European Union through the EU4Health programme, and was also supported by Gavi
View five news elements
Time
The assessment took place on 17–18 August 2026, with the measles outbreak beginning in early 2023 and ongoing activities in October 2024.
Location and population
The assessment was conducted in Bishkek, Kyrgyzstan, involving national public health organizations and international partners across the country.
Event
WHO, in collaboration with the Kyrgyzstan Ministry of Health and the International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies, conducted an assessment of the measles outbreak response in Kyrgyzstan.
Scale, impact and risk
Kyrgyzstan remains among the 10 countries in the WHO European Region with the highest number of reported measles cases. Four rounds of supplementary immunization activities were organized, with coverage rates of 85%, 79%, 88%, and 74% respectively.
Response, status and uncertainty
The assessment identified immediate and longer-term corrective actions, developed an action plan, and integrated measles preparedness into wider health security planning. The fourth round of immunization is still ongoing.

The Iowa HHS and local health officials have identified these locations and are urging anyone present during the specified time to monitor for measles symptoms. If exposed and symptomatic, individuals are advised to contact their medical providers before arriving to avoid spreading the virus further. The response includes monitoring for symptoms and promoting vaccination as the best prevention method. The outbreak is currently under investigation, and the public is encouraged to follow health guidelines to protect their community.

威斯康星州确诊的 麻疹 病例达到 15 例 - FOX6 News 密尔沃基

Confirmed measles cases reach 15 in Wisconsin - FOX6 News Milwaukee
新闻简报威斯康星州卫生当局于 2026 年 8 月 15 日报告了 15 例确诊的 麻疹 病例,比三天前的两例大幅上升,表明拉斐特县和爱荷华州当地 传播 病例活跃。威斯康星州卫生服务部与当地和疾病预防控制中心团队合作,正在追踪暴露情况并试图确定感染源,同时警告可能还会出现更多未发现的病例。麻疹 是一种通过空气传播的病毒,在未接种疫苗的接触者中二次感染率为 90%,造成严重后果的风险很高,包括住院和死亡。公共卫生官员敦促立即使用两剂 MMR 疫苗(有效率 97%),并建议通过州登记处检查免疫记录。暴发 强调了更广泛的国家担忧,因为美国 麻疹 疫苗接种 覆盖率已降至预防 传播 所需的 95% 阈值以下,威胁到该国 2000 年的消除状态。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 17 日 12:25 CDT;事件期间至少从8月12日(2例确诊病例)到2026年8月17日(15例确诊病例)。
地点与对象
美国威斯康星州;特别是拉斐特县和爱荷华县;受影响人群包括未接种疫苗的个体和 疫苗接种 状态不完整的个体。
事件
威斯康星州卫生服务部 (DHS) 于 2026 年 8 月 15 日确认了 15 例 麻疹 病例,高于 2026 年 8 月 12 日的 2 例,表明 麻疹 病例正在迅速扩大。该事件已得到证实并正在积极调查中。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年8月15日,确诊麻疹病例15例,疑似病例6例。由于当地传播活跃且传染性极高(无保护接触者的续发攻击率高达90%),存在大量未被发现病例的风险。麻疹为气溶胶传播病毒,可能引发严重并发症,包括住院(每4例中有1例)和死亡(每1,000名儿童中最多2例死亡)。
应对、状态与不确定性
威斯康星州国土安全部、当地卫生官员和疾病预防控制中心正在调查、追踪接触情况并遏制传播。未确定公共暴露地点;感染源仍在保密调查中。DHS 为 疫苗接种 (MMR) 提供建议,通过威斯康星州免疫登记处检查免疫记录,并联系医疗保健提供者获取加强剂的指导。目前状态正在调查中,来源识别尚未确定。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Confirmed measles cases reach 15 in Wisconsin - FOX6 News Milwaukee

News Brief
Wisconsin health authorities reported 15 confirmed measles cases on 15 August 2026, a sharp rise from just two cases three days earlier, signaling active local transmission in Lafayette and Iowa counties. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services, working with local and CDC teams, is tracing exposures and attempting to identify the infection source while warning that additional undetected cases are likely. Measles, an airborne virus with a 90% secondary infection rate among unvaccinated contacts, poses high risks of severe outcomes, including hospitalization and death. Public health officials urge immediate vaccination with two doses of the MMR vaccine, which is 97% effective, and advise checking immunization records via the state registry. The outbreak underscores broader national concerns, as U.S. measles vaccination coverage has fallen below the 95% threshold needed to prevent transmission, threatening the country’s 2000 elimination status.
Original source text (English)
Confirmed measles cases reach 15 in Wisconsin | FOX6 Milwaukee LIVE News Weather Sports WakeUp Things To Do Contests More Watch Live Expand / Collapse search ☰ Search site News Local Wisconsin's Most Wanted FOX6 Investigators Entertainment Podcasts Politics FOX6 News Insta-Poll FOX LOCAL mobile app FOX6 en Español Site Map Weather Severe weather alerts FOX6 Storm Center app FOX6 Live Cam Network FOX Weather app FOX Weather Closings Future Forecaster FOX6 Snow Stick webcam Money Contact 6 FOX6 Cents Recalls Personal Finance Business Small Business WakeUp Submit for Look Who's 6 Look Who's 6 Real Milwaukee Out and About Interviews Food Gino At The Movies Milwaukee Traffic Tracker Sports Milwaukee Brewers Green Bay Packers Beyond The Game Milwaukee Admirals High School Hot Shots Milwaukee Bucks Watch LIVE How to stream LIVE FOX6 newscasts Wis Live Desk: ICYMI FOX6 TV Listings LiveNOW from FOX FOX Weather FOX Sports FOX Soul FOX News Sunday FOX Shows FOX6 Social Media YouTube Facebook Instagram TikTok Threads Bluesky X (formerly Twitter) Facebook (FOX6 Weather) X (FOX6 Weather) Community Things To Do Beer Garden Finder Fish Fry Finder Buddy Check 6 Project ADAM Community Connection Regional, Live News Chicago News - FOX 32 Minneapolis News - FOX 9 Detroit News - FOX 2 LiveNOW from FOX Contact Us Contests Sign up for email alerts FOX6 Mobile Apps FOX6 News Team Jobs at FOX6 EEO Report Public File Help FCC Applications Closed Captioning Info DTV Reception Questions Sales & Advertising FOX6 Sales Team Advertise with FOX6 FOX FOCUS Sponsored content Stream on FOX One Where to watch NFL Where to watch College Football Where to watch MLB Where to watch NASCAR Cup Series Where to watch IndyCar Where to watch Men's College Basketball Where to watch Women's College Basketball Where to watch WNBA Games Where to watch MLS Games Severe Thunderstorm Watch until FRI 10:00 PM CDT, Jefferson County, Walworth County, Kenosha County Confirmed measles cases reach 15 in Wisconsin By Maureen Breslin FOX6 News Milwaukee Health Published August 17, 2026 12:25 PM CDT Share Copy Link Email Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Reddit Published August 17, 2026 12:25 PM CDT article Box of measles mumps and rubella vaccines ( (Photo by Joe Raedle/Getty Images) / Getty Images) MILWAUKEE, Wis. (FOX 6) - The Wisconsin Department of Health Services confirmed 15 cases of measles Thursday, with an additional 6 probable cases. This was a jump from 2 confirmed cases last Tuesday. Measles outbreak in Lafayette and Iowa Counties What we know: According to Dr. Ryan Westergaard with the Wisconsin DHS, the department is working closely with local health officials in Lafayette and Iowa counties, as well as getting national support from the Centers of Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) to investigate the cases, trace exposure and contain further spread. What we don't know: At this time, the Wisconsin DHS states it has not identified any public exposure locations and is currently confidentially contacting those who have developed measles to trace the source of the disease. The rapid emergence of cases among those who have not traveled outside Wisconsin suggests there is active local transmission, Westergaard explained. This makes additional cases within the community that have not yet been identified or reported to public health services likely. Preventing measles What you can do: The best thing to do to prevent measles transmission is to receive the MMR vaccine. Two doses of the measles vaccine are 97% effective at preventing the disease, while one dose is 93% effective at preventing measles, according to the CDC. You can check your vaccine status using the Wisconsin Immunization Registry, which is an online database that tracks vaccine records for Wisconsinites. You can also contact your healthcare provider. Guidance on whether you should receive a measles vaccine booster is also provided by the Wisconsin DHS. If you were born before 1957, you do not need a measles booster because you were likely already exposed to the disease. If you were vaccinated between 1957-1989 you may benefit from a measles booster. Some vaccines used between 1963 and 1967 were inactive. You should contact your healthcare provider for more guidance. If you were vaccinated after 1989, you have likely received two doses of the measles vaccine and have effective protection against the disease. DHS is also asking people to be familiar with the symptoms of measles. If you do go to a healthcare provider, it is important that you call them in advance to inform them of your potential measles status, so that they can take precautions to prevent further spread of the disease. Measles symptoms Dig deeper: Measles is an extremely contagious disease. If one person has measles, up to 90% of people who come into close contact with that person will become infected if they are not protected, according to the CDC. The disease spreads when an infected person coughs or sneezes, making it an airborne virus. A person can contract measles simply by being in a room where an infected person with measles has been up to two hours after the person has already left the room. You can also get measles by touching an infected surface and then touching your eyes, nose or mouth. This means that the disease can be contracted anywhere in public life. Measles symptoms: Symptoms of measles typically begin to develop 10 to 21 days after infection. Symptoms include: Runny nose High fever (could be above 104 degrees Fahrenheit) Fatigue Cough Red, watery eyes, known as conjunctivitis, or "pink eye" A red rash with raised bumps that starts at the hairline and moves to the arms and legs three to five days after symptoms begin Rash that spreads all over the body, usually beginning three to five days after initial symptoms Measles complications: One out of every four people who get measles in the U.S. will be hospitalized, and up to two out of every 1,000 children who contract measles in the U.S. will die, even with advanced healthcare. These are other groups of people at higher risk of experiencing severe measles complications: Children younger than 5 years old Adults older than 20 years old Pregnant women Those with weakened immune systems, such as from leukemia or HIV Serious health complications that can come as a result of measles infection include pneumonia, brain damage caused by swelling, and deafness. While common complications include ear infections and diarrhea. Measles cases on the rise The backstory: Measles outbreaks have been on the rise in recent years. In 2025, the U.S. had more reported measles cases, outbreaks, affected states, and deaths than in any year since 1992, according to a study by KFF, a nonpartisan health policy research organization. Of the measles cases in the U.S. contracted between 2025-2026, over 90% of the cases were seen in unvaccinated people. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) National measles vaccination levels have also decreased over the past five years, with kindergarten and childhood measles coverage rates dipping to 92.5%, well below the 95% threshold needed to prevent transmission of measles virus. In Wisconsin, the CDC cites during the 2024-2025 school year, only about 85% of kindergartners in the state were vaccinated against measles, ranking lower than the national average. Map showing measles vaccination rates across the U.S. with data from the CDC. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) Measles was declared eliminated from the United States in 2000, according to the CDC. This means there was no continuous spread of measles for longer than 12 months. The U.S. is on the verge of losing its measles elimination status, as Canada did in late 2025, according to The Associated Press. Routine childhood vaccination schedules executive order Big picture view: President Donald Trump announced an executive order last week aimed at childhood vaccinations that would require separating combination shots, including the measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine into separate injections. During the signing of his order, Trump repeated false claims about vaccinations, including their relationship to autism and that the MMR vaccine could be lethal, according to the AP. Trump's executive order calls for federal officials to develop plans within 90 days for breaking up the MMR shot and spacing out other vaccines. However, pharmaceutical scientists and former regulators contend that those revisions would take years and require drug manufacturers to spend millions of dollars on new studies and manufacturing facilities. Many public health experts were quick to condemn the president's executive order, according to the AP. Dr. Paul Offit, a Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia vaccine researcher and former government adviser, said the recommendations by Trump and Health and Human Services Sec. Robert F. Kennedy Jr."are just not science based," and it’s irresponsible to cast doubt on a vaccine that protects against measles when the nation is facing its biggest epidemic in more than 30 years, according to the AP. Right now, there are no individual vaccines in the U.S. for measles, mumps or rubella. Every vaccine approved for those viruses by the Food and Drug Administration is combination shots. Local perspective: The Wisconsin DHS Secretary put out a statement on Thursday regarding the president's executive order, continuing to back previous guidance from public health officials that supports the current recommended MMR vaccine. "There is no new scientific evidence that justifies changing recommendations that have, and continue to, protect children across the United States," wrote DHS Secretary Kirsten Johnson. In February, Wisconsin was among states that sued the Trump administration over previous changes to federal childhood vaccine recommendations. The Source: Information from this article was sourced from the Wisconsin Department of Health Services, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, KFF, and the Associated Press. This story was reported from Eden Prairie, Minnesota. Time
Published 17 August 2026 at 12:25 CDT; event period spans from at least 12 August (2 confirmed cases) to 17 August 2026 (15 confirmed cases).
Location and population
Wisconsin, USA; specifically Lafayette and Iowa counties; affected population includes unvaccinated individuals and those with incomplete vaccination status.
Event
The Wisconsin Department of Health Services (DHS) confirmed 15 measles cases on 15 August 2026, up from 2 cases on 12 August 2026, indicating a rapidly expanding outbreak. The event is confirmed and under active investigation.
Scale, impact and risk
15 confirmed measles cases and 6 probable cases as of 15 August 2026. Risk of additional undetected cases is high due to active local transmission and high contagiousness (up to 90% secondary attack rate among unprotected contacts). Measles is an airborne virus with potential for severe complications, including hospitalization (1 in 4 cases) and death (up to 2 per 1,000 children).
Response, status and uncertainty
Wisconsin DHS, local health officials, and CDC are investigating, tracing exposures, and containing spread. No public exposure locations identified; source of infection remains under confidential investigation. DHS advises vaccination (MMR), checking immunization records via the Wisconsin Immunization Registry, and contacting healthcare providers for guidance on boosters. Current status is under investigation with unresolved source identification.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

State health officials confirm 12 new measles cases in Iowa - The Des Moines Register

News Brief
Iowa health officials confirmed 12 new measles cases in 2026, raising the state’s total to 13, with the outbreak concentrated in southern Iowa. The surge follows a national trend: 2,170 U.S. cases have been reported so far this year, already nearing 2025’s total of 2,289, which included 243 hospitalizations and three deaths. Public health authorities attribute the rise to plummeting vaccination rates, noting that 90% of infections occur in unvaccinated individuals. The Iowa Department of Health and Human Services is coordinating with local agencies to trace contacts, provide vaccinations, and warn exposed residents to monitor for symptoms for up to three weeks.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Opinion Business Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals IOWA State health officials confirm 12 new measles cases in Iowa Norah Judson Des Moines Register Aug. 17, 2026Updated Aug. 18, 2026, 7:09 p.m. CT (This story was updated because it contained an inaccuracy.) The Iowa Department of Health and Human Services has confirmed 12 new cases of measles in the state, bringing the total for 2026 to 13. Last year, the state reported just nine cases. According to a Monday, Aug. 17, news release, the outbreak is in southern Iowa. DHHS believes person-to-person transmission has played a role in the spread of the highly contagious illness and said it is working with local public health agencies to ensure affected families and communities have access to care and vaccination. Places and times infected individuals could have spread measles DHHS says the infected individuals visited: Natural Grocers, 8650 Hickman Road, Clive, between 2:30 and 5 p.m. July 28. Vero Health Center, 630 S 50th St., West Des Moines, between 3:30 and 6:30 p.m. July 28. Office Depot, 3910 University Ave., West Des Moines, between 5 and 7:30 p.m. July 28. Menards, 12000 Hickman Road, Clive, between 6 and 8:30 p.m. July 28. Menards, 6000 SE 14th St., Des Moines, between 7 and 9:30 p.m. July 28. Wells Hometown Drug, 208 E Franklin St., Bloomfield, between 4:30 and 7 p.m. July 30. JR Feed and Supply, 17599 200th St., Bloomfield, between 8:30 a.m. and 6 p.m. Aug. 9. What is measles and what are the symptoms? Measles is a viral illness with symptoms that include fever, cough, red and watery eyes, a runny nose and a rash. Symptoms may appear from seven to 14 days after contact with someone who is infected and contagious. The virus is spread through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes and breathes and can remain virulent for up to two hours. According to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 90% of people who come into contact with the virus become infected if they are unvaccinated, making it one of the most contagious diseases known. Additionally, 1 in 5 of those people end up hospitalized, according to the CDC. Measles can cause health complications including ear infections and diarrhea, as well as pneumonia, swelling of the brain and in some cases, death. Children are most at risk for serious complications. In 2025, there was a total of nine measles cases in Iowa and 2,289 across the country, with 243 patients hospitalized and three deaths, according to the DHHS. This year is set to outpace 2025, with 2,170 confirmed cases so far nationwide and more than four months remaining. All but 10% of infections occurred in people who were unvaccinated or had unknown vaccination status. How can I avoid getting measles? Measles is a vaccine-preventable disease. The MMR vaccine (measles, mumps and rubella) protects against measles with 97% efficacy, and is typically administered to children as part of the regular vaccine course, with the first dose when the child is 12 to 15 months of age and the second at 4 to 6 years. "The routine childhood immunization series is still our strongest protection against measles infection," Dr. Robert Kruse, director of the DHHS, said in the release. The recent increase in measles outbreaks has been attributed to plummeting vaccination rates. What to do if you believe you are infected with measles Those who may have been exposed to the infected individual are asked to monitor for symptoms for up to 21 days and to call their medical provider or the nearest emergency room before arrival and tell them they have been exposed to measles and have symptoms. CORRECTION: A previous version of this article had an incomplete number of measles cases in Iowa. The southern Iowa outbreak contains 12 cases, making 13 total cases in Iowa for 2026. USA Today staff writer Mary Walrath-Holridge and Des Moines Register staff writer Kyle Werner contributed to this article. Norah Judson is a staff writer for the Register. Penn State Health notifies public of potential measles exposure at Penn State Health Golisano Children’s Hospital - Penn State Health News

News Brief
Penn State Health issued a public notice on August 21, 2026, regarding a potential measles exposure at Golisano Children’s Hospital. While no confirmed cases have been reported, the alert highlights the risk of measles virus (Morbillivirus hominis) exposure among pediatric patients and the broader community. The hospital is investigating the incident, though details on testing, isolation protocols, or contact tracing remain undisclosed. Measles, a highly contagious viral disease, poses significant health risks, particularly to unvaccinated individuals. This notification underscores the importance of vaccination and public health vigilance. Further updates are anticipated as the investigation progresses.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

威斯康星州 麻疹 病例达到 36 例,过去一周增加了一倍多 - FOX6 News 密尔沃基

Wisconsin measles cases reach 36, more than doubling in past week - FOX6 News Milwaukee
新闻简报截至 2026 年 8 月 18 日,威斯康星州卫生当局报告了 36 例 麻疹 确诊病例和 3 例疑似病例,是一周前确诊的 15 例的两倍多。所有病例都与威斯康星州西南部的拉斐特县、格兰特县和爱荷华县有关联并集中,没有州外旅行史,表明当地活跃的 传播。威斯康星州卫生服务部在疾病预防控制中心的支持下正在进行接触者追踪和暴露调查,同时对暴露地点保密。公共卫生官员强调,未接种疫苗的个人仍然面临最高风险,并指出两剂 MMR 疫苗 的有效率为 97%。麻疹 具有高度传染性,通过空气中的飞沫或受污染的表面传播,并可能导致严重的并发症,包括住院和死亡,特别是对于幼儿和免疫功能低下的个体。

时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 18 日 17:24 CDT;活动期间至少为2026年8月11日至18日。
地点与对象
美国威斯康星州;拉斐特县、格兰特县和爱荷华州确诊病例;未接种疫苗的人是高危人群。
事件
威斯康星州卫生服务部 (DHS) 确认了 36 例 麻疹 病例和 3 例疑似病例,较一周前的 15 例增加了一倍;在 CDC 的支持下,调查仍在进行中,以追踪接触情况并遏制传播。
规模、影响与风险
确诊病例36例,疑似病例3例;一周内病例数快速增长(从15例增至36例);未接种疫苗者风险最高;麻疹传染性极强,在未采取防护措施的接触者中继发攻击率高达90%;并发症包括美国病例中1/4需住院治疗,以及每1000名感染儿童中最多2例死亡。
应对、状态与不确定性
威斯康星州国土安全部正在与当地卫生官员和疾病预防控制中心合作,调查、追踪接触情况并遏制进一步传播;尚未确定公共暴露地点;国土安全部正在秘密联系案件以追踪来源;指南强调MMR 疫苗接种,两剂有效率97%;如果怀疑存在 麻疹,DHS 建议在就诊前致电医疗保健提供者。

Wisconsin measles cases reach 36, more than doubling in past week - FOX6 News Milwaukee

News Brief
Wisconsin health authorities reported 36 confirmed measles cases and three probable cases as of 18 August 2026, more than doubling the 15 cases confirmed a week earlier. All cases are linked and concentrated in Lafayette, Grant, and Iowa counties in southwestern Wisconsin, with no travel history outside the state, indicating active local transmission. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services, supported by the CDC, is conducting contact tracing and exposure investigations while keeping exposure sites confidential. Public health officials stress that unvaccinated individuals remain at highest risk, noting that two MMR vaccine doses are 97% effective. Measles is highly contagious, spreading via airborne droplets or contaminated surfaces, and can cause severe complications including hospitalization and death, particularly in young children and immunocompromised individuals.

Wisconsin measles cases reach 36, more than doubling in past week | FOX6 Milwaukee LIVE News Weather Sports WakeUp Things To Do Contests More Watch Live Expand / Collapse search ☰ Search site News Local Wisconsin's Most Wanted FOX6 Investigators Entertainment Podcasts Politics FOX6 News Insta-Poll FOX LOCAL mobile app FOX6 en Español Site Map Weather Severe weather alerts FOX6 Storm Center app FOX6 Live Cam Network FOX Weather app FOX Weather Closings Future Forecaster FOX6 Snow Stick webcam Money Contact 6 FOX6 Cents Recalls Personal Finance Business Small Business WakeUp Submit for Look Who's 6 Look Who's 6 Real Milwaukee Out and About Interviews Food Gino At The Movies Milwaukee Traffic Tracker Sports Milwaukee Brewers Green Bay Packers Beyond The Game Milwaukee Admirals High School Hot Shots Milwaukee Bucks Watch LIVE How to stream LIVE FOX6 newscasts Wis Live Desk: ICYMI FOX6 TV Listings LiveNOW from FOX FOX Weather FOX Sports FOX Soul FOX News Sunday FOX Shows FOX6 Social Media YouTube Facebook Instagram TikTok Threads Bluesky X (formerly Twitter) Facebook (FOX6 Weather) X (FOX6 Weather) Community Things To Do Beer Garden Finder Fish Fry Finder Buddy Check 6 Project ADAM Community Connection Regional, Live News Chicago News - FOX 32 Minneapolis News - FOX 9 Detroit News - FOX 2 LiveNOW from FOX Contact Us Contests Sign up for email alerts FOX6 Mobile Apps FOX6 News Team Jobs at FOX6 EEO Report Public File Help FCC Applications Closed Captioning Info DTV Reception Questions Sales & Advertising FOX6 Sales Team Advertise with FOX6 FOX FOCUS Sponsored content Stream on FOX One Where to watch NFL Where to watch College Football Where to watch MLB Where to watch NASCAR Cup Series Where to watch IndyCar Where to watch Men's College Basketball Where to watch Women's College Basketball Where to watch WNBA Games Where to watch MLS Games Severe Thunderstorm Watch until FRI 10:00 PM CDT, Jefferson County, Walworth County, Kenosha County Wisconsin measles cases reach 36, more than doubling in past week By Maureen Breslin FOX6 News Milwaukee Health Published August 18, 2026 5:24 PM CDT Share Copy Link Email Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Reddit Published August 18, 2026 5:24 PM CDT article (Photo by Joe Raedle/Getty Images) ((Photo by Joe Raedle/Getty Images) / Getty Images) The Brief The Wisconsin Department of Health Services has confirmed that measles cases in the state have risen to 36 confirmed cases and three probable cases. These cases have been confirmed in Lafayette, Grant and Iowa counties and are all related. Those who are unvaccinated remain the most at risk for measles. MILWAUKEE - The Wisconsin Department of Health Services has now confirmed 36 measles cases as of Tuesday. This is a jump up from 15 cases that were confirmed last Thursday. More measles cases confirmed in Grant County What we know: In addition to the previous outbreaks in Lafayette and Iowa counties, two cases have been confirmed in Grant County, according to the Wisconsin DHS. There are also three additional probable cases in the area. These cases are all related to the prior cases within the southwestern Wisconsin region and bring the total known outbreak numbers to 36. According to Dr. Ryan Westergaard with the Wisconsin DHS, the department is working closely with local health officials in Lafayette and Iowa counties, as well as getting national support from the Centers of Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) to investigate the cases, trace exposure and contain further spread. What we don't know: At this time, the Wisconsin DHS states it has not identified any public exposure locations and is currently confidentially contacting those who have developed measles to trace the source of the disease. The rapid emergence of cases among those who have not traveled outside Wisconsin suggests there is active local transmission, Westergaard explained. This makes additional cases within the community that have not yet been identified or reported to public health services likely. Preventing measles What you can do: The best thing to do to prevent measles transmission is to receive the MMR vaccine. Two doses of the measles vaccine are 97% effective at preventing the disease, while one dose is 93% effective at preventing measles, according to the CDC. You can check your vaccine status using the Wisconsin Immunization Registry, which is an online database that tracks vaccine records for Wisconsinites. You can also contact your healthcare provider. Guidance on whether you should receive a measles vaccine booster is also provided by the Wisconsin DHS. If you were born before 1957, you do not need a measles booster because you were likely already exposed to the disease. If you were vaccinated between 1957-1989 you may benefit from a measles booster. Some vaccines used between 1963 and 1967 were inactive. You should contact your healthcare provider for more guidance. If you were vaccinated after 1989, you have likely received two doses of the measles vaccine and have effective protection against the disease. DHS is also asking people to be familiar with the symptoms of measles. If you do go to a healthcare provider, it is important that you call them in advance to inform them of your potential measles status, so that they can take precautions to prevent further spread of the disease. Measles symptoms Dig deeper: Measles is an extremely contagious disease. If one person has measles, up to 90% of people who come into close contact with that person will become infected if they are not protected, according to the CDC. The disease spreads when an infected person coughs or sneezes, making it an airborne virus. A person can contract measles simply by being in a room where an infected person with measles has been up to two hours after the person has already left the room. You can also get measles by touching an infected surface and then touching your eyes, nose or mouth. This means that the disease can be contracted anywhere in public life. Measles symptoms Symptoms of measles typically begin to develop 10 to 21 days after infection. Symptoms include: Runny nose High fever (could be above 104 degrees Fahrenheit) Fatigue Cough Red, watery eyes, known as conjunctivitis, or "pink eye" A red rash with raised bumps that starts at the hairline and moves to the arms and legs three to five days after symptoms begin Rash that spreads all over the body, usually beginning three to five days after initial symptoms Measles complications One out of every four people who get measles in the U.S. will be hospitalized, and up to two out of every 1,000 children who contract measles in the U.S. will die, even with advanced healthcare. These are other groups of people at higher risk of experiencing severe measles complications: Children younger than 5 years old Adults older than 20 years old Pregnant women Those with weakened immune systems, such as from leukemia or HIV Serious health complications that can come as a result of measles infection include pneumonia, brain damage caused by swelling, and deafness. While common complications include ear infections and diarrhea. Measles cases on the rise The backstory: Measles outbreaks have been on the rise in recent years. In 2025, the U.S. had more reported measles cases, outbreaks, affected states, and deaths than in any year since 1992, according to a study by KFF, a nonpartisan health policy research organization. Of the measles cases in the U.S. contracted between 2025-2026, over 90% of the cases were seen in unvaccinated people. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) National measles vaccination levels have also decreased over the past five years, with kindergarten and childhood measles coverage rates dipping to 92.5%, well below the 95% threshold needed to prevent transmission of measles virus. In Wisconsin, the CDC cites during the 2024-2025 school year, only about 85% of kindergartners in the state were vaccinated against measles, ranking lower than the national average. Exemptions are on the rise as well. For the 2025-2026 school year, 8.2% of Wisconsin parents got exemptions for their children to not receive vaccinations, and the vast majority of these exemptions were non-medical. Map showing measles vaccination rates across the U.S. with data from the CDC. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) Measles was declared eliminated from the United States in 2000, according to the CDC. This means there was no continuous spread of measles for longer than 12 months. The U.S. is on the verge of losing its measles elimination status, as Canada did in late 2025, according to The Associated Press. Routine childhood vaccination schedules executive order Big picture view: President Donald Trump announced an executive order last week aimed at childhood vaccinations that would require separating combination shots, including the measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine into separate injections. During the signing of his order, Trump repeated false claims about vaccinations, including their relationship to autism and that the MMR vaccine could be lethal, according to the AP. Trump's executive order calls for federal officials to develop plans within 90 days for breaking up the MMR shot and spacing out other vaccines. However, pharmaceutical scientists and former regulators contend that those revisions would take years and require drug manufacturers to spend millions of dollars on new studies and manufacturing facilities. Many public health experts were quick to condemn the president's executive order, according to the AP. Dr. Paul Offit, a Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia vaccine researcher and former government adviser, said the recommendations by Trump and Health and Human Services Sec. Robert F. Kennedy Jr."are just not science based," and it’s irresponsible to cast doubt on a vaccine that protects against measles when the nation is facing its biggest epidemic in more than 30 years, according to the AP. Right now, there are no individual vaccines in the U.S. for measles, mumps or rubella. Every vaccine approved for those viruses by the Food and Drug Administration is combination shots. Local perspective: The Wisconsin DHS Secretary put out a statement on Thursday regarding the president's executive order, continuing to back previous guidance from public health officials that supports the current recommended MMR vaccine. "There is no new scientific evidence that justifies changing recommendations that have, and continue to, protect children across the United States," wrote DHS Secretary Kirsten Johnson. In February, Wisconsin was among states that sued the Trump administration over previous changes to federal childhood vaccine recommendations. The Source: Information from this article was sourced from the Wisconsin Department of Health Services, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, KFF, and the Associated Press. This story was reported from Eden Prairie, Minnesota. HealthWisconsinNews

麻疹 暴发 威斯康星州西南部确诊病例增至 36 例,疑似病例增至 3 例 - WPR

Measles outbreak grows to 36 confirmed, 3 probable cases in southwest Wisconsin - WPR
新闻简报据威斯康星州卫生服务部称,截至 2026 年 8 月 18 日,威斯康星州西南部的 麻疹 暴发 病例已扩大至 36 例确诊病例和 3 例疑似病例。大多数病例涉及 18 岁以下未接种疫苗的儿童,据报道有两例住院治疗。公共卫生团队正在进行接触者追踪并提供暴露后 疫苗接种,但尚未确定任何公共暴露地点。州官员警告说,威斯康星州幼儿园 MMR 疫苗接种 率为 85.1%,达不到预防 暴发 所需的高覆盖率,并指出当前的事件是几年来最大规模的一次。卫生当局敦促居民通过威斯康星州免疫登记处核实免疫状况,如果未受到保护,则收到 疫苗接种。
查看新闻五要素
时间
事件期间:2026 年 8 月(首次报告于 8 月 14 日;当前更新于 2026 年 8 月 18 日)。发布日期:2026年8月18日下午5点27分。
地点与对象
威斯康星州西南部,包括爱荷华县、拉斐特县和格兰特县。受影响人群:主要是17岁及以下儿童;除一名感染者外,所有人均未接种疫苗。据报道有两人住院治疗。
事件
威斯康星州卫生服务部 (DHS) 确认了 麻疹 暴发,截至 2026 年 8 月 18 日,已有 36 例确诊病例和 3 例疑似病例。当地卫生官员正在州 DHS 的支持下积极调查 麻疹。
规模、影响与风险
确诊病例36例,疑似病例3例,住院治疗2例。麻疹 通过空气传播的 传播 具有高度传染性,可导致严重的并发症,包括肺炎、脑损伤、耳聋和死亡。2025-2026 年威斯康星州幼儿园 MMR 疫苗接种 覆盖率为 85.1%,低于群体免疫阈值。
应对、状态与不确定性
国土安全部和县卫生部门正在进行访谈、协助检测并提供暴露后 疫苗接种。尚未确定公共暴露地点。州官员指出,暴发 的规模比近年来更大,并强调 疫苗接种 作为一级预防工具。正在进行调查以确定暴露程度。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles outbreak grows to 36 confirmed, 3 probable cases in southwest Wisconsin - WPR

News Brief
A measles outbreak in southwest Wisconsin has expanded to 36 confirmed and three probable cases as of August 18, 2026, according to the Wisconsin Department of Health Services. The majority of cases involve unvaccinated children under 18, with two hospitalizations reported. Public health teams are conducting contact tracing and offering post-exposure vaccinations, while no public exposure sites have been identified. State officials warn that Wisconsin’s kindergarten MMR vaccination rate of 85.1% falls short of the high coverage needed to prevent outbreaks, noting the current event is the largest in several years. Health authorities urge residents to verify immunization status via the Wisconsin Immunization Registry and receive vaccination if unprotected.
Original source text (English)

Mary Conlon/AP Photo The measles outbreak in southwestern Wisconsin has grown to 36 confirmed and three probable cases. Last week, the Wisconsin Department of Health Services said they were investigating 15 confirmed and six probable measles cases in Iowa County and Lafayette County. A Tuesday statement from the department said there are now two confirmed measles cases in Grant County as well. “Public health officials across each county continue the work to identify and notify people who may have been exposed to the measles virus,” the statement said. “At this time, no public exposure locations have been identified in connection with these cases.” Understanding Wisconsin, Together. WPR’s “Wisconsin Today” newsletter keeps you connected to the state you love without feeling overwhelmed. No paywall. No agenda. No corporate filter. Company This field is for validation purposes and should be left unchanged. Email Dr. Ryan Westergaard, chief medical officer with the Wisconsin DHS Bureau of Communicable Diseases, said a DHS team has been meeting multiple times a day to assist the county public health departments. He said local health officials are in charge of the investigation into the outbreak and have been doing interviews and facilitating testing. “And also giving vaccines out to people who are potentially exposed and at risk and don’t know their immunization status,” Westergaard said. The outbreak has been growing quickly. State officials first reported two confirmed cases in Lafayette and Iowa counties on Aug. 11. In the initial notification, state officials said the two people confirmed to have the virus appeared to have contracted it in Wisconsin “suggesting there may be additional measles cases in communities that have not yet been identified.” Two days later, the number of confirmed cases had grown to 15. According to state data, all but one of the people infected in the state this year have been unvaccinated. The majority are children 17 and under. Two people have been hospitalized. Measles is highly contagious and can be spread through the air. The state DHS statement said it, “can stay in the air for two hours after a sick person coughs or sneezes.” “Measles can cause serious health complications, including pneumonia, brain damage, and deafness, and can sometimes be deadly,” the statement said. The outbreak comes as new data from the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention showed Wisconsin had low measles vaccination rates for kindergarten students. An estimated 85.1 percent of kindergarten students in Wisconsin had MMR vaccine coverage during the 2025-2026 school year, according to the CDC data. “We would like that to be higher,” Westergaard said. “The measles virus is one of the most contagious viruses that we have,” he added. “So it takes really high levels of vaccine coverage to prevent outbreaks when a case is present.” Westergaard said the rate of vaccination for the virus has gotten to a “dangerously low level in this country.” He said there’s only been small measles outbreaks in Wisconsin over the last two years. “But the current one is larger than we’ve had in the past several years,” he said. “So we’re working really hard to try to understand the extent, meaning how many people have been exposed and get the resources that they need, which is primarily vaccination to prevent the ongoing spread of measles in this community.” Two doses of the measles vaccine is 97 percent effective at preventing the virus, while one dose is 93 percent effective, according to the CDC. Westergaard encouraged people to check the Wisconsin Immunization Registry to see if they are up to date. “The best time to get vaccinated is before you’re exposed, but this is one of the vaccines that we can actually use after exposure too to prevent the likelihood of getting sick from measles,” Westergaard said. Southwest Wisconsin, including Iowa County, Lafayette County, and Grant County. Affected population: predominantly children 17 years and under; all but one infected person unvaccinated. Two hospitalizations reported.
Event
A measles outbreak confirmed by the Wisconsin Department of Health Services (DHS), with 36 confirmed and 3 probable cases as of August 18, 2026. The outbreak is under active investigation by local health officials with state DHS support.
Scale, impact and risk
36 confirmed cases, 3 probable cases, 2 hospitalizations. Measles is highly contagious via airborne transmission and can cause serious complications including pneumonia, brain damage, deafness, and death. Wisconsin kindergarten MMR vaccination coverage is 85.1% for 2025-2026, below herd immunity thresholds.
Response, status and uncertainty

DPH Confirms Measles Case in Georgia - MMR Vaccine is Safe and Effective in Preventing Measles - Georgia Coastal Health District
新闻简报乔治亚州公共卫生部 (DPH) 于 2026 年 8 月 20 日确认了一名未接种疫苗的亚特兰大都会区居民的 麻疹 病例。卫生当局强调了 MM​​R 疫苗 在预防 麻疹 方面的安全性和有效性。官方通知中没有提供有关暴露地点、接触者或公共卫生干预措施的进一步细节。该病例是乔治亚州今年报告的首例确诊的 麻疹 感染,促使人们呼吁在全球 麻疹 卷土重来的风险中遵守 麻疹 规定。DPH 没有透露患者的年龄、临床状况或潜在的暴露时间表。

DPH Confirms Measles Case in Georgia - MMR Vaccine is Safe and Effective in Preventing Measles - Georgia Coastal Health District

News Brief
The Georgia Department of Public Health (DPH) confirmed a measles case in an unvaccinated metro Atlanta resident on August 20, 2026. Health authorities emphasized the safety and effectiveness of the MMR vaccine in preventing measles. No further details on exposure sites, contacts, or public health interventions were provided in the official notice. The case marks the first confirmed measles infection reported in Georgia this year, prompting calls for vaccination compliance amid ongoing global measles resurgence risks. DPH did not disclose the patient's age, clinical status, or potential exposure timeline.

Health officials talk Measles after four confirmed cases in Meigs County - WTAP
新闻简报俄亥俄州梅格斯县公共卫生当局于 2026 年 8 月 18 日确认了四例活跃的 麻疹 病例,促使俄亥俄州中部山谷地区追踪接触者并提高警惕。卫生官员警告说,麻疹 是最具传染性的空气传播病毒之一,感染者离开后能够在室内空气中残留长达两个小时。症状通常在接触后 7-21 天出现,首先是发烧、咳嗽和皮疹,并可能导致肺炎和脑炎等严重并发症,特别是在幼儿、老年人和免疫功能低下的个体中。大约五分之一的感染者需要住院治疗。当局敦促居民核实 疫苗接种 的状况,如果未接种疫苗,请避免前往受影响地区,并在就医之前联系医疗服务提供者,以防止进一步传播。

俄亥俄州梅格斯县公共卫生官员报告了四例确诊的 麻疹 病例,并启动了接触者追踪;该事件已得到证实并正在积极调查中。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health officials talk Measles after four confirmed cases in Meigs County - WTAP

News Brief
Public health authorities in Meigs County, Ohio, confirmed four active measles cases on August 18, 2026, prompting contact tracing and heightened vigilance across the Mid-Ohio Valley. Health officials warn that measles is among the most contagious airborne viruses, capable of lingering in indoor air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves. Symptoms typically appear 7–21 days post-exposure, beginning with fever, cough, and rash, and can lead to severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, particularly in young children, older adults, and immunocompromised individuals. Approximately one in five infected persons requires hospitalization. Authorities urge residents to verify vaccination status, avoid travel to affected areas if unvaccinated, and contact providers before seeking care to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)

(WTAP) - Public health officials are urging residents in the Mid-Ohio Valley to remain vigilant following the confirmation of four active measles cases in Meigs County, Ohio. The Meigs County Health Department (MCHD) is currently tracing contacts and notifying individuals who may have been exposed. Local health agencies are warning that while there are currently no suspected cases or contacts under monitoring in West Virginia’s Mid-Ohio Valley, the region’s proximity and busy summer travel season increase the potential for cross-border transmission. Amy Phelps, Public Information Officer for the Mid-Ohio Valley Health Department, emphasized that travel is the primary driver of regional risk. “With the summer and people traveling, there’s always a chance that they could travel to an area where someone has measles,” Phelps said. “And if they don’t have immunity to it, they could catch it.” A Highly Contagious Airborne Threat Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known, spreading rapidly through person-to-person respiratory droplets. Health officials warn that the virus is exceptionally resilient, capable of lingering in the air of an enclosed room for up to two hours after an infected individual has left. Symptoms typically develop 7 to 14 days after exposure, though they can take up to 21 days to appear. The initial symptoms closely mimic other viral infections, starting with a high fever, cough, runny nose, and watery or red eyes similar to conjunctivitis. “About a couple days after those, then a lot of times people can get the white spots in the roof of their mouth,” Phelps explained. “And then a couple days after that is when the rash can start. And it usually starts on the head and then spreads downward.” Medical guidelines require infected individuals to remain isolated until at least five days after the initial appearance of the rash, at which point the virus is no longer transmissible. High Risks for Vulnerable Populations According to the Meigs County Health Department, approximately 20% of individuals who contract measles require hospitalization due to severe complications. These secondary infections occur because the virus severely weakens the body’s immune system. “Serious complications for measles is usually like younger children, generally five and under,” Phelps said. “And then older adults, people with compromised immune systems... they’re more apt to have secondary infections like pneumonia.” Other potential complications include severe dehydration and swelling of the brain (encephalitis). In pregnant women who have not received the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine, contracting the virus can lead to premature birth or low birth weight. Testing and Prevention Protocols Public health departments do not offer direct testing or treatment for measles. If residents suspect they have been exposed and begin experiencing symptoms, health officials warn against walking directly into clinics or emergency departments. “You don’t want people just showing up at the door generally,” Phelps said, noting that local healthcare clinics and hospitals have strict isolation protocols to prevent spreading the airborne pathogen. Patients are instructed to contact their primary care physician or hospital before arriving so proper precautions can be taken. For those unsure of their vaccination status, healthcare providers can perform a blood titer test to check for active antibodies. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends the MMR vaccine be administered in two doses: the first between 12 and 15 months of age, and the second between ages 4 and 6. Officials also noted a distinct demographic advantage for older residents: individuals born before 1957 are widely considered to have lifetime natural immunity to measles due to widespread exposure prior to the development of the vaccine. For families traveling with unvaccinated children or immunocompromised members, health department officials strongly advise monitoring outbreak databases and avoiding areas with documented active cases. US measles cases surpass 2,000 for the 2nd year in a row: CDC - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

US measles cases surpass 2,000 for the 2nd year in a row: CDC - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos
新闻简报根据 CDC 数据,截至 2026 年 8 月,美国 麻疹 病例已连续第二年超过 2,000 例,38 个州和华盛顿特区报告了 2,030 例确诊病例。暴发 于 2026 年 1 月开始,对未接种疫苗的儿童和青少年造成了不成比例的影响,92% 的病例发生在未接种疫苗或 疫苗接种 状态未知的个人中。去年共有 2,288 例病例,其中包括该国十多年来首次出现的 麻疹 死亡病例。卫生官员警告称,持续的 传播 可能会危及美国的 麻疹 消除状态,泛美卫生组织定于 2026 年 11 月进行严格审查。幼儿园儿童的 疫苗接种 覆盖率已降至 92.5%,低于大流行前的水平,促使人们呼吁加强公共卫生信息传递和提供者参与以应对 疫苗犹豫。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-19;该事件将持续到 2026 年,并于 2026 年 1 月首次发现,并计划于 2026 年 11 月进行消除状态审查。
地点与对象
美国(38 个州和哥伦比亚特区);大多数病例是 19 岁及以下的儿童和青少年;92% 的病例未接种疫苗或 疫苗接种 状态未知。
事件
麻疹 麻疹 美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 报告称,2026 年确诊病例将超过 2,000 例,这是连续第二年超过这一阈值;状态正在进行中并正在调查中。
规模、影响与风险
2026年确诊病例2,030例;2025年确诊病例2,288例;2026年国际旅行者中出现10例麻疹病例;2025 年,十多年来首次出现 麻疹 死亡(三例死亡:德克萨斯州两名未接种疫苗的学龄儿童和新墨西哥州一名未接种疫苗的成年人);如果连续 传播 被确认,则有失去美国 麻疹 消除资格的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC 建议接种两剂 MMR 疫苗(第一剂在 12-15 个月时接种,第二剂在 4-6 岁时接种);2024-2025学年,幼儿园儿童疫苗接种覆盖率下降至92.5%;正在进行调查以确定 传播 是否已连续发生 12 个月;泛美卫生组织定于 2026 年 11 月进行消除状况审查;公共卫生专家强调通过与医疗保健提供者对话来解决 疫苗 的犹豫不决。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

US measles cases surpass 2,000 for the 2nd year in a row: CDC - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
U.S. measles cases have exceeded 2,000 for the second straight year, with 2,030 confirmed cases reported across 38 states and Washington, D.C. as of August 2026, according to CDC data. The outbreak, which began in January 2026, has disproportionately affected unvaccinated children and teenagers, with 92% of cases occurring in individuals who are unvaccinated or whose vaccination status is unknown. Last year’s total of 2,288 cases included the nation’s first measles deaths in over a decade. Health officials warn that sustained transmission could jeopardize the U.S. measles elimination status, with a critical review by the Pan-American Health Organization slated for November 2026. Vaccination coverage among kindergartners has declined to 92.5%, below pre-pandemic levels, prompting calls for strengthened public health messaging and provider engagement to counter vaccine hesitancy.
Original source text (English)
So far this year, 2,030 cases have been recorded in 38 states and D.C. ABC News medical correspondent Dr. Darien Sutton discusses the latest in the state's outbreak, which started last October. Measles outbreak in South Carolina is over, health officials say ABC News medical correspondent Dr. Darien Sutton discusses the latest in the state's outbreak, which started last October. Measles outbreak in South Carolina is over, health officials say ABC News medical correspondent Dr. Darien Sutton discusses the latest in the state's outbreak, which started last October. Measles outbreak in South Carolina is over, health officials say ABC News medical correspondent Dr. Darien Sutton discusses the latest in the state's outbreak, which started last October. Measles cases in the United States have surpassed 2,000 for the second year in a row, according to data updated Friday from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). So far in 2026, 2,030 confirmed cases have been recorded in 38 states and the District of Columbia, CDC data shows. Cases have been confirmed in: Alaska, Arizona, California, Colorado, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Illinois, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Texas, Utah, Vermont, Virginia, Washington, Wisconsin and Wyoming. Just 10 measles cases were reported among international travelers so far this year, according to CDC data. Last year, 2,288 confirmed measles cases were reported for all of 2025. Prior to this, measles cases had not surpassed 2,000 in the U.S. since 1992. The U.S. also saw its first measles deaths in more than a decade last year, including two among unvaccinated school-aged children in Texas and one among an unvaccinated adult in New Mexico. The measles virus. BSIP/Universal Images Group via Getty Images The majority of measles cases in 2026 have been confirmed among children and teenagers aged 19 and younger, according to the CDC. About 92% of cases are among people who are unvaccinated or whose vaccination status is unknown, CDC data shows. Meanwhile, 4% of cases are among those who have received just one dose of the measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine and 4% of cases are among those who received the recommended two doses, according to the CDC. Dr. William Schaffner, a professor of preventive medicine at Vanderbilt University Medical Center in Nashville, referred to the latest figures as "very disappointing and very concerning." "It indicates that an increasing number of parents are either postponing or withholding their children from vaccination, and this is very concerning because it permits this virus back now into in the United States to continue to spread and obviously to cause illness in the children affected," he told ABC News. January 2026 marked one year since measles cases were first detected on Texas. It’s unclear if the cases confirmed on Jan. 20, 2025, are linked to those that have been found other states; if so, it would mean the U.S. has seen a year of continuous transmission. If it's determined that the U.S. has experienced 12 months of continuous measles transmission, it could lead to a loss of the country's elimination status that was earned in 2000. Measles would once again be considered endemic or constantly circulating. The review of the measles elimination status in the U.S., which is determined by the Pan-American Health Organization, will take place in November 2026. Schaffner said the U.S.'s measles elimination status is threatened as a result of so many cases. "Measles, because it's the most contagious virus, is like the canary in the coal mine; it's the alert that lets us know that we're also opening ourselves up to other transmissible diseases that vaccines could prevent," he said. The CDC currently recommends that people receive two doses of the MMR vaccine, the first at ages 12 to 15 months and the second between 4 and 6 years old. One dose is 93% effective, and two doses are 97% effective against measles, the CDC says. However, CDC data show vaccination rates have been lagging in recent years. During the 2024 to 2025 school year, 92.5% of kindergartners received the MMR vaccine, according to data. This is lower than the 92.7% seen the previous school year and the 95.2% seen in the 2019 to 2020 school year, prior to the COVID-19 pandemic. Schaffner said that public health specialists need to listen carefully to parents who are skeptical or hesitant of getting their children vaccinated. "Have them speak to their own family doctors and pediatricians, have those conversations, and our pediatricians and family doctors have to provide a level of comfort and reassurance," he said. "Facts are fundamental, but more importantly, these parents need reassurance and a level of comfort that what their doctors ... are recommending is in the best interest of their child and the communities in which their children live." "These diseases are bad and can be really bad. The vaccines are good and really good," Schaffner added

Event
Measles outbreak reported by the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) as surpassing 2,000 confirmed cases in 2026, marking the second consecutive year above this threshold; status is ongoing and under investigation.
Scale, impact and risk
2,030 confirmed cases in 2026; 2,288 confirmed cases in 2025; 10 measles cases among international travelers in 2026; first measles deaths in over a decade in 2025 (three deaths: two unvaccinated school-aged children in Texas and one unvaccinated adult in New Mexico); risk of losing U.S. measles elimination status if continuous transmission is confirmed.
Response, status and uncertainty
CDC recommends two doses of MMR vaccine (first dose at 12–15 months, second dose at 4–6 years); vaccination coverage among kindergartners declined to 92.5% in 2024–2025 school year; investigation ongoing to determine if continuous transmission has occurred for 12 months; elimination-status review by Pan-American Health Organization scheduled for November 2026; public health specialists emphasize addressing vaccine hesitancy through dialogue with healthcare providers.

明尼苏达州幼儿园 麻疹 疫苗接种 率全美第三低 - MPR News

Minnesota kindergartners have third lowest measles vaccination rate in U.S. - MPR News
新闻简报The article reports that Minnesota kindergartners have the third lowest measles vaccination rate in the U.S., with an MMR rate of 86 percent. This decline, noted since 2020 and accelerated in the last year, raises concerns about herd immunity, which requires at least 90 percent vaccination coverage to protect vulnerable groups. The drop is linked to anti-vaccination campaigns targeting immigrant communities and shifting public sentiment. Minnesota also has a high exemption rate of 6.9 percent. Dr. Andrea Singh, a pediatrician, highlights the dangers of measles, which is highly contagious and can lead to severe complications like subacute sclerosing panencephalitis. She encourages parents with vaccine concerns to consult medical professionals for accurate information and reassurance. The report underscores the need for maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent outbreaks and protect public health.

该文章发表于2026年8月20日。疫苗接种费率自2020年以来一直在持续下降,去年下降明显。
地点与对象
The report focuses on Minnesota, specifically kindergartners in the state. It mentions that Minnesota has one of the highest exemption rates at 6.9 percent.
事件
Minnesota kindergartners have the third lowest measles vaccination rate in the U.S., with a reported rate of 86 percent for MMR vaccines. This decline is attributed to factors including anti-vaccination campaigns targeting immigrant communities and public sentiment shifts.
规模、影响与风险
The article highlights the risk of losing herd immunity, which requires at least 90 percent vaccination rates to protect vulnerable populations. It also notes that measles is highly contagious and can lead to serious complications like subacute sclerosing panencephalitis.
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员担心 疫苗接种 率下降以及群体免疫力的潜在丧失。Andrea Singh 强调咨询值得信赖的医疗服务提供者并通过对话解决 疫苗 问题的重要性。

Minnesota kindergartners have third lowest measles vaccination rate in U.S. - MPR News

News Brief
The article reports that Minnesota kindergartners have the third lowest measles vaccination rate in the U.S., with an MMR rate of 86 percent. This decline, noted since 2020 and accelerated in the last year, raises concerns about herd immunity, which requires at least 90 percent vaccination coverage to protect vulnerable groups. The drop is linked to anti-vaccination campaigns targeting immigrant communities and shifting public sentiment. Minnesota also has a high exemption rate of 6.9 percent. Dr. Andrea Singh, a pediatrician, highlights the dangers of measles, which is highly contagious and can lead to severe complications like subacute sclerosing panencephalitis. She encourages parents with vaccine concerns to consult medical professionals for accurate information and reassurance. The report underscores the need for maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent outbreaks and protect public health.
