Save Share As kids start looking ahead to the new school year, health officials are looking at new immunization data — and it’s showing rates have continued to fall over the last year especially in Minnesota. Kindergartners in our state have the second and third lowest vaccination levels in the country for DTaP and MMR, respectively, at 86 percent for both. These two vaccines are combination shots that protect against a myriad of diseases like whooping cough and measles. Minnesota also has one of the highest exemption rates at 6.9 percent. Dr. Andrea Singh, chair of pediatrics at HealthPartners and Park Nicollet, said these numbers reflect what she’s been seeing in her office. Turn Up Your Support MPR News helps you turn down the noise and build shared understanding. Turn up your support for this public resource and keep trusted journalism accessible to all. “More and more in a clinic setting, we’re getting a lot of parents that are questioning the need for vaccinations or the motivation behind vaccines.” She said there are numerous factors that could be leading to the decline but noted in Minnesota particularly there was a campaign in the late 1990s and 2000s. “There was a very targeted effort by some anti-vaccination groups to talk to immigrant communities and make some unfounded links between MMR vaccines, in particular, and the likelihood that your child would have autism.” The major concern from health officials as rates fall for the MMR vaccination is the loss of what’s called “herd immunity.” That’s when at least 90 percent of the population is vaccinated in order to protect those who haven’t been vaccinated or more vulnerable populations. Dr. Singh said what many might not know is that measles is one of the most contagious and dangerous diseases. One person with measles could spread it to 10 to 12 people if they’re breathing the same air. It can also lead to other serious illness like subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, a degenerative and almost always deadly brain disease that appears years after recovery from measles. Earlier this month, President Donald Trump signed an executive order calling for the revamping of the childhood vaccine schedule. As the public sentiment and policies around vaccinations continue to shift, Dr. Singh said it’s important for people — especially those who may have concerns about vaccines — to talk with a trusted medical provider. “Ask the questions, don’t be scared,” Dr. Singh. “Ask and we can help work with you on helping to make you comfortable. But we know 100 percent that vaccines, in general, are safe and they save lives.” Use the audio player above to listen to the full conversation. The article was published on August 20, 2026. The decline in vaccination rates has been ongoing since 2020, with a notable drop in the last year.
Location and population
The report focuses on Minnesota, specifically kindergartners in the state. It mentions that Minnesota has one of the highest exemption rates at 6.9 percent.
Event
Minnesota kindergartners have the third lowest measles vaccination rate in the U.S., with a reported rate of 86 percent for MMR vaccines. This decline is attributed to factors including anti-vaccination campaigns targeting immigrant communities and public sentiment shifts.
Scale, impact and risk
The article highlights the risk of losing herd immunity, which requires at least 90 percent vaccination rates to protect vulnerable populations. It also notes that measles is highly contagious and can lead to serious complications like subacute sclerosing panencephalitis.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health officials are concerned about the declining vaccination rates and the potential loss of herd immunity. Andrea Singh emphasizes the importance of consulting trusted medical providers and addressing vaccine concerns through dialogue.

邻近县 麻疹 病例增加 - 汤普金斯周刊

Measles cases on rise in neighboring counties - Tompkins Weekly
新闻简报纽约州北部的公共卫生官员警告称,邻近的泰奥加县和塞内卡县的 麻疹 病例有所增加,促使汤普金斯县整体卫生部门敦促居民核实其 麻疹 状态。麻疹 是一种高度传染性的呼吸道病毒,对未接种疫苗的人,特别是婴儿、幼儿、孕妇和免疫系统较弱的人,会带来严重的风险,包括肺炎、脑肿胀和永久性残疾。MMR 疫苗 的有效率仍为 97%,但即使在接种疫苗的人群中,仍然存在较小的感染风险。当局建议,如果接触过病毒,请立即联系医疗保健提供者,并在未事先通知的情况下避免前往急诊室或诊所,以防止进一步的 传播。强调常规儿童免疫接种计划,建议在 12-15 个月大时接种第一剂 MMR,在 4 至 6 岁时接种第二剂。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 18 日;事件报告为正在进行中,证据中没有提供具体的事件日期。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases on rise in neighboring counties - Tompkins Weekly

News Brief
Public health officials in upstate New York are warning of a rise in measles cases in neighboring Tioga and Seneca counties, prompting Tompkins County Whole Health to urge residents to verify their vaccination status. Measles, a highly contagious respiratory virus, poses severe risks—including pneumonia, brain swelling, and permanent disabilities—for unvaccinated individuals, particularly infants, young children, pregnant persons, and those with weakened immune systems. The MMR vaccine remains 97% effective, though a small risk of infection persists even in vaccinated people. Authorities advise contacting healthcare providers immediately if exposed and avoiding emergency rooms or clinics without prior notice to prevent further transmission. Routine childhood immunization schedules are emphasized, with the first MMR dose recommended at 12–15 months and the second between ages 4 and 6.
Original source text (English)
Friday, August 2180°F / Ithaca Sign IneNewsletter News Sports Business Education Lifestyle Opinion Spotlight Events Submit Your Event Public Notices Sign In eNewsletter Directory E-Editions Obituaries Advertise Event Submission Hometown Heroes Hometown Heroes Submission Made in Tompkins News Sports Opinion Business Education Lifestyle Spotlight Events Newsletter News August 18, 2026 Measles cases on rise in neighboring counties Measles cases are rising in Tioga and Seneca counties, prompting Tompkins County Whole Health to urge residents to ensure they are fully vaccinated. Tompkins County Whole Health (TCWH) is alerting the community that measles cases are on the rise in neighboring counties, including Tioga and Seneca Counties. Measles is a highly contagious virus and spreads rapidly among those who are unvaccinated. Being unvaccinated increases risks for severe complications, hospitalization and death. Those most at risk of infection include unvaccinated infants, children or adults (excluding those who were born before 1957, who are considered immune), pregnant persons, and those with weakened immune systems. TCWH urges everyone to be vaccinated against measles. A complete vaccination (two-dose series of MMR (Measles, Mumps, Rubella) vaccine) is safe and effective at preventing infection. The measles virus is easily spread through the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes or by direct contact with a contaminated surface. Symptoms can take from 7 days up to 21 days from exposure to occur. Measles can often be mistaken for other respiratory illnesses, as symptoms include a high fever, cough, runny or congested nose, sore throat and watery eyes. A red and blotchy rash may occur within 3-5 days following the start of cold-like symptoms, with the rash usually appearing on the face first. The rash may spread down the neck, trunk, arms, legs and feet, and spots may join together as they spread. When the rash appears, fever temperatures may rise above 104° Fahrenheit. If you suspect you were exposed to measles, contact our Division of Community Health at 607-274-6604. Post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) for measles may be given within 72 hours of exposure to prevent infection or lessen the severity of the disease in people who do not have immunity and were exposed to the virus. If you develop symptoms, please call your healthcare provider or contact our office. Let the provider or answering service know that you have been exposed to measles or what symptoms you are experiencing. Please do not go to the Emergency Room, Urgent Care, or a doctor’s office without first calling to avoid potentially exposing other people to measles. Because measles can spread quickly and easily through the air, it is very important to seek advice over the phone first to reduce risk of further spreading the virus. This also gives healthcare facilities an opportunity to take safety measures, such as placing you in an isolated room immediately, to prevent spreading the virus to other vulnerable patients in the waiting room or other areas. Join Our Community: Free eNewsletters Sign-UpStay in the know and receive weekly updates, breaking news, and engaging stories directly in your inbox every Wednesday and Friday by subscribing to our eNewsletters. Do not fill this field Sign Up Verified Complications from measles are serious and include very high fever, ear infections, pneumonia, and encephalitis (swelling of the brain). Risks also include permanent loss of hearing, vision, and the development of learning disabilities. There is no cure for measles, only treatment for symptom relief. Measles is especially dangerous for those who are unvaccinated, for young children, and for those who are immunocompromised. TCWH Medical Director Dr. William Klepack stated, “Measles is a terrible virus with a very high infection rate in unvaccinated populations. The MMR vaccine is 97% effective at preventing infection. This means that while the vaccine is very effective at preventing illness, there is a risk that 3 out of every 100 vaccinated people could still become infected. The risk if you are not vaccinated is much greater.” Dr. Klepack continued, “We strongly encourage families to make sure their children, especially those under age 5, stay up to date on their vaccination schedule. Children should get their first MMR shot at 12 through 15 months old (as soon as possible within this time-period). The second dose may be given as soon as one month after the first dose but is typically given between 4 and 6 years of age. At this time, there is no need to seek early vaccination for your infant and maintaining the routine immunization schedule is advised.” TCWH advises all who have not been vaccinated against measles or have had only one dose to contact their healthcare provider or our office to get up to date with their vaccinations. Measles Used to Be in Our Rearview Mirror. Now Cases Are Reaching Record Highs. - ProPublica

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles Used to Be in Our Rearview Mirror. Now Cases Are Reaching Record Highs. - ProPublica

News Brief
The United States is experiencing a record measles resurgence, with over 4,850 cases and three deaths reported since early 2025, including two unvaccinated girls in West Texas. As of July 23, 2026, case numbers have surpassed any year since 1991, signaling a sharp reversal of decades of progress. Public health experts warn that measles, one of the most contagious diseases, is re-emerging as vaccination rates decline and policy shifts sow doubt about vaccine safety. Hospitals have responded by isolating measles patients to curb airborne transmission, which can persist for up to two hours. The crisis reflects broader vulnerabilities in immunization systems, with officials fearing renewed outbreaks of other preventable diseases. While federal agencies deny restricting vaccine access, concerns persist over potential changes to programs that could further destabilize supply.
Original source text (English)

About UsImpactLeadershipStaffPartners Contact UsPressSend Us Tips Topics We Cover See All Abortion Civil Rights Courts Criminal Justice Debt Democracy Education Climate and Environment Health Care Health Insurance Immigration Labor Mental Health Military Police Politics Pregnancy Prison Racial Justice Regulation Sex and Gender Technology Our Biggest Series See All Life of the Mother How Abortion Bans Lead to Preventable Deaths The New Immigration How Recent Arrivals at the Border Have Changed the Country and Its Attitudes Friends of the Court SCOTUS Justices’ Beneficial Relationships With Billionaire Donors Hospitals set up special testing sites to prevent measles patients from spreading the disease in waiting rooms. The virus can linger in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves. Jan Sonnenmair/Getty Images Measles Used to Be in Our Rearview Mirror. Now Cases Are Reaching Record Highs. In other parts of the world that have let their guard down to vaccine-preventable diseases, long-forgotten scourges have roared back and killed and disabled children. by Patricia Callahan August 18, 2026, 5:00 am Change Appearance AutoLightDark The U.S. recently reached a grim milestone: As of July 23, more measles cases have been reported to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention than in any year since 1991. It took less than seven months to blow past the three-decade high recorded last year. This potentially deadly disease is easily prevented. Shots are widely available and, in many cases, free. Still, since the beginning of last year, more than 4,850 people here have been infected, and three have died. Among them were two unvaccinated girls in West Texas whose deaths made national headlines. It may feel like the continuous stream of cases is just the way things are now. But it is dangerous to shrug off the resurgence of measles. In a story I reported earlier this year, I showed what’s happened in parts of the world that have let their guard down to vaccine-preventable diseases. My reporting found that long-forgotten scourges roared back, killing and disabling children. Even countries that made what seemed like subtle changes to immunization policies suffered disastrous consequences years later. Read More How Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s Vaccine Agenda Risks a Resurgence of Deadly Childhood Plagues Babies were born blind, deaf and with intellectual disabilities because their mothers caught rubella in the early weeks of pregnancy. Parents saw why diphtheria used to be known as “the strangling angel of children.” Doctors told me they couldn’t undo the damage when these horrors returned. Modern medicine can do a lot of things, but it can’t reverse paralysis from polio. The story also showed how Robert F. Kennedy Jr., the nation’s top health official, and President Donald Trump have been transforming a government that for decades promoted the lifesaving benefits of shots into one that sows doubts about their safety. Last week, the president issued another executive order that’s sure to fuel further hesitancy about the shot that has protected children from measles, mumps and rubella for decades. It’s a sharp departure for a government that, since the 1960s and with bipartisan support, built vaccination programs that shielded kids from deadly and disabling diseases. Federal and state health leaders strengthened those policies based on lessons learned from measles outbreaks. That’s because measles, among the most contagious diseases in history, is almost always the first vaccine-preventable disease to strike when immunization rates fall. Some U.S. schools have set up vaccination clinics as immunization rates have dropped in many parts of the country. Sarah L. Voisin/The Washington Post via Getty Images In my many months of reporting for that story, I learned that measles is like the bright dye that plumbers flush through pipes to find leaks in a complex system. The continued appearance of the virus’ telltale rash in state after state since last year warns of cracks in our once-solid protection, leaving communities vulnerable to other deadly contagions. My reporting also found that access to vaccines may be in peril: Since last year, Kennedy has been considering changes to an obscure but crucial government program that could prompt the few companies that make vaccines for American kids to flee the U.S. market. If that were to happen, even parents who still want vaccines for their kids wouldn’t be able to get them. A spokesperson for the Department of Health and Human Services said the agency has not limited access to vaccines. In an email, the spokesperson wrote, “Secretary Kennedy believes that trust is rebuilt through an open review of safety data, the willingness to ask the hard questions, and ensuring the American people have all emerging information as soon as we know it.” Several dozen people who’ve worked on U.S. government vaccination programs here and around the world conveyed to me a sense of foreboding about America. They fear that children will wind up fighting for their lives against infections that have long been preventable. “I think there always was a worst-case scenario,” said Dr. Melinda Wharton, who retired last September after more than three decades leading CDC immunization programs. “I don’t think I imagined it could or would be this bad.” Doctors I spoke to told me they had seen images of those diseases in textbooks, but these were problems medicine had solved long ago. They never thought they’d see an actual patient until an unvaccinated child lay before them, gravely ill with one of these ancient scourges. I’ll never forget what one German doctor, whose hospital treated an unvaccinated boy who later died of diphtheria, told me of the infection: “It was taught as history,” he said. Here in the U.S., measles was taught as history, too. Now it isn’t. We’ve been covering public health and vaccine policies for years. Let me walk you through reporting from my colleagues and me that helps show how we ended up here and where we might go. Oct. 15, 2020 Inside the Fall of the CDC In 2020, I reported with my colleagues on how the world’s greatest public health organization was brought to its knees by COVID-19, the president and the capitulation of its own leaders. And we also foreshadowed how the government’s response to COVID-19 led to a loss of trust in public health institutions — a lingering resentment that RFK Jr. has tapped into. (The CDC and the White House declined to comment on this story.) March 28, 2025 The CDC Buried a Measles Forecast That Stressed the Need for Vaccinations At the beginning of the second Trump administration, I learned that CDC leaders ordered staff not to release their experts’ assessment that found the risk of catching measles was high in areas near outbreaks where vaccination rates were lagging. In an aborted plan to roll out the news, the agency would have emphasized the importance of vaccinating people against the disease that had then infected fewer than 500 people in 19 states, the records show. (At the time, the CDC told us that the agency decided against releasing the assessment “because it does not say anything that the public doesn’t already know” and that the agency continued to recommend vaccines as “the best way to protect against measles.”) July 17, 2025 RFK Jr. Wants to Change a Program That Stopped Vaccine Makers From Leaving the U.S. Market. They Could Flee Again. Not long after I broke the news about the buried measles forecast, I learned more about a vaccine program that RFK Jr. plans to overhaul, one that’s vital to Americans’ access to immunizations. I showed how dramatic changes to this little-known program — which offers payouts for people who suffer rare side effects from shots without having to prove that drugmakers were negligent — could prompt vaccine makers to stop selling them here. (HHS declined to answer detailed questions about Kennedy’s plans. Previously, an HHS spokesperson had said that Kennedy is “not anti-vaccine — he is pro-safety.”) That story laid bare how surprisingly fragile the nation’s vaccine system is. Kennedy still hasn’t revealed what he plans to do. March 27, 2026 What Could Happen if Americans Who Wanted Vaccines Couldn’t Get Them? Two Stanford epidemiologists had modeled the scenario. My colleague Lucas Waldron created a chilling visualization that allowed us to depict how many people could die or be disabled in 25 years if vaccines for polio, measles, rubella or diphtheria were no longer available. ProPublica shared the key findings of that scenario with HHS. An agency spokesperson didn’t address the modeling but said “HHS has not limited access or insurance coverage to any FDA-approved vaccines” and routinely recommends the shots for children. June 8, 2026 What ProPublica Found in the Genetic Code of America’s Measles Outbreaks As measles cases continued to rise, a team at the CDC was investigating whether the disease has become endemic in this country again. In June, my colleague Nat Lash and I showed how hard it will be for them to prove that it isn’t, after what Nat found when he analyzed the genetic code of cases around the country. A CDC spokesperson told ProPublica that the agency was conducting a more comprehensive investigation and that genetic sequences “alone cannot determine whether” measles is endemic. 暴发 发生在纽约州北部,特别是在斯托本县、塞内卡县、麦迪逊县和阿勒格尼县。受影响的人群包括未接种疫苗的个人以及参加过马匹拍卖并穿越俄亥俄州和宾夕法尼亚州的人。
事件
纽约州北部报告了 6 例确诊的 麻疹 病例和 14 例推测感染者。卫生部门发现,马匹拍卖的参加者以及来自俄亥俄州和宾夕法尼亚州的旅行者存在潜在的共同暴露风险。暴发 与 疫苗接种 费率下降和 疫苗 豁免增加有关。
规模、影响与风险
有 6 名确诊的 麻疹 病例和 14 名疑似感染者。麻疹病毒 具有高度传染性,未接种疫苗的个体有 90% 的机会感染 麻疹病毒。正如 CDC 指出的,麻疹 威胁到了美国 麻疹 的消除状况。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生部门正在进行接触者追踪并确定潜在的接触者。CDC 已宣布 麻疹 在美国被淘汰,但 麻疹 强调了由于 疫苗接种 率低而重新流行的风险。目前情况正在调查中,并正在监测暴发是否进一步传播。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Six confirmed measles cases, 14 presumed infected across upstate NY - WXXI News

News Brief
A recent outbreak of measles has been reported in upstate New York, with six confirmed cases and 14 presumed infected individuals. The cases were identified in Steuben, Seneca, Madison, and Allegany Counties, with a potential common exposure linked to horse auctions and travel through Ohio and Pennsylvania. The outbreak comes as vaccination rates decline, with over 4% of parents opting out of nonmedical exemptions for their children's kindergarten vaccinations. Public health officials warn that measles, a highly contagious virus, poses a significant risk to unvaccinated individuals, with a 90% chance of transmission in close contact. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) declared measles eliminated in the U.S. in 2000, but the resurgence highlights the dangers of vaccine hesitancy. The health department is actively tracing contacts and monitoring the situation for further spread. This outbreak underscores the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent the virus from re-establishing itself in the population.
Original source text (English)

Four upstate New York counties have reported measles cases over the past week. This news comes a few weeks before school begins, and at a time when more parents are opting their children out of vaccinations. On Tuesday, Steuben County confirmed one case of measles. Another case was confirmed in Seneca County on Aug. 14. As of Aug. 14, Madison County had one confirmed case in an unvaccinated resident. And Allegany County confirmed Wednesday that three individuals were infected with the virus and 14 others were presumed to be infected. The Allegany County health department said they identified a potential common exposure among individuals who attended horse auctions and traveled through Ohio and Pennsylvania. Erie County reported two measles cases in early June. Measles is a highly contagious virus that can cause severe illness or death. "If a person who is not immune — that is, they've never had measles and they've never been vaccinated — if they come in contact with someone who has measles in the same room, there is a 90% chance or higher that they will get measles," said Dr. Paul Graman, an epidemiologist at Strong Memorial Hospital. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention declared measles eliminated in the United States in 2000, but dropping vaccination rates are now threatening that status. According to Graman, public health has become the victim of its own success. He said because the measles virus had all but disappeared for decades, some people question the need to get vaccinated. "Having the attitude that, 'I'm protected or not protected' or 'I don't care,' or 'I've never seen measles,' how are you going to feel if you get measles, and then someone in your school who can't be vaccinated, a child, gets a serious case of measles, and it could have been prevented?" he asked. New CDC data show over 4% of parents opted their children out of routine vaccinations for nonmedical reasons before they entered kindergarten last year. That exemption rate is an all-time high. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Outbreak of measles confirmed in Kentucky; officials report hospitalizations - WAVE News

News Brief
A confirmed measles outbreak is ongoing in multiple counties of Kentucky, including Christian, Todd, Trigg, and Washington Counties. State health officials reported at least two hospitalizations linked to the outbreak as of August 17, 2026. The measles virus is highly contagious and can lead to serious complications, including permanent health issues, damage, or death, according to the World Health Organization. Officials are investigating the outbreak and have urged residents to check their immunization status and get the MMR vaccine if necessary. Updated county-by-county case counts are published every weekday morning on the state health department's official website. The outbreak is the largest in Kentucky in 35 years, highlighting the importance of vaccination in preventing the spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)

(WAVE) - State health officials are investigating a confirmed and ongoing outbreak of measles across multiple Kentucky counties. As of Monday, Aug. 17, confirmed measles cases have been found in Christian, Todd, Trigg and Washington Counties. Officials say that the outbreak has resulted in at least two hospitalizations. The virus that causes the measles rash is highly contagious and can have serious secondary complications that include permanent health issues, damage or death. These complications can happen in 30 to 40% of cases, according to the World Health Organization. In 2024, a reported 95,000 people died from measles around the world. “Vaccination is the best protection from measles,” KDPH said in a public statement. “The measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine is safe, effective, and widely available in Kentucky.” Health officials urge residents to check their immunization status and get the MMR vaccine if their immunity has lapsed or they have not previously received the vaccine. To receive the MMR vaccine, residents should contact their primary care physician, a local health care provider, or their county health department. The state health department publishes updated county-by-county case counts every weekday morning on its official website. Copyright 2026 WAVE. All rights reserved. 什么麻疹?亚利桑那州的孩子们以创纪录的速度跳过 疫苗接种 - Axios

What measles? Arizona kids are skipping vaccinations at a record rate - Axios
新闻简报文章报道称,2025-2026 学年,亚利桑那州的 麻疹 案件大幅增加,幼儿园儿童的 疫苗 豁免创下历史新高。根据疾病控制和预防中心的数据,DTaP 和 MMR 疫苗 的 疫苗接种 率下降至 87.4%,其中 10.6% 的幼儿园儿童获得豁免。自 2025 年初以来,暴发 已导致 344 例确诊病例和 27 例住院治疗,其中大多数病例集中在科罗拉多市。公共卫生官员警告说,将 疫苗接种 率维持在 95% 以上对于群体免疫至关重要。亚利桑那州卫生服务部正在通过努力减少障碍并增加获得 疫苗 的机会来解决这一问题。疫苗 的支持者对豁免激增表示担忧,这可能与特朗普政府最近的政策变化有关。这些变化,包括修改儿童 疫苗 时间表以及将 MMR 疫苗 分成三个单独的镜头,预计将面临法律挑战。情况仍在调查中,卫生部门的重点是建立对 疫苗 和公共卫生举措的信任。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于2026-08-20。自2025年初起,亚利桑那州报告了344例麻疹病例,其中27例住院。自2025年初至今,马里科帕县今年已报告27例麻疹病例,为至少2006年以来最多。
地点与对象
亚利桑那州,特别是该州和马里科帕县。受影响的人群包括幼儿园学生和更广泛的社区,10.6% 的幼儿园学生在上学年获得了至少一项 疫苗 的豁免。
事件
2025-2026 学年,亚利桑那州幼儿园儿童的 疫苗接种 率有所下降,但豁免率升至历史新高。这与 麻疹 暴发 相吻合,包括 2025 年以来的 344 例病例和 27 例住院治疗。疾病控制和预防中心报告了这些发现。
规模、影响与风险
亚利桑那州的 DTaP 和 MMR 疫苗 的 麻疹 疫苗接种 比率下降至 87.4%。自 2025 年以来,麻疹 暴发 已导致 344 例病例和 27 例住院治疗,其中大多数病例集中在科罗拉多市。公共卫生官员强调,疫苗接种 率应保持在 95% 以上,以维持群体免疫力。
应对、状态与不确定性
亚利桑那州卫生服务部正在与合作伙伴合作,减少 疫苗接种 的障碍并增加获取机会。疫苗 的支持者对豁免激增表示担忧,特朗普政府的政策变化可能会进一步削弱对 疫苗 的信任。目前情况仍在调查中,需要继续开展公共卫生工作。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

What measles? Arizona kids are skipping vaccinations at a record rate - Axios

News Brief
The article reports that Arizona has seen a significant rise in measles cases and a record high in vaccine exemptions among kindergartners during the 2025–2026 school year. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, vaccination rates for DTaP and MMR vaccines dropped to 87.4%, with 10.6% of kindergarteners obtaining exemptions. The outbreak has resulted in 344 confirmed cases and 27 hospitalizations since the start of 2025, with most cases concentrated in Colorado City. Public health officials warn that maintaining vaccination rates above 95% is essential for herd immunity. The Arizona Department of Health Services is addressing the issue by working to reduce barriers and increase access to vaccines. Vaccine advocates have expressed concern over the exemption spike, which may be linked to the Trump administration's recent policy changes. These changes, including a revised childhood vaccine schedule and breaking the MMR vaccine into three separate shots, are expected to face legal challenges. The situation remains under investigation, and the health department is focused on building trust in vaccines and public health initiatives.

Add Axios on Google Illustration: Sarah Grillo/Axios Vaccination rates among kindergartners in Arizona decreased during the 2025–2026 school year for all tracked vaccines, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported on Monday, while vaccine exemptions rose to their highest rate since at least 2012. Why it matters: The drop-off coincided with continued efforts by the Trump administration to make controversial vaccine policy changes and with measles cases in Arizona hitting levels not seen in at least two decades. By the numbers: Coverage with the diphtheria, tetanus and acellular pertussis (DTaP) and measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccines bottomed out at 87.4% for each. Public health officials say overall measles vaccination rates should remain above 95% to maintain herd immunity. 10.6% of kindergarteners — roughly 7,800 kids — obtained an exemption from at least one vaccine the last school year, the third-highest exemption rate for any state. The vast majority of those exemptions were for non-medical reasons. Context: In Arizona, parents can claim exemptions for medical, religious and personal reasons. Data: CDC; Note: Data collection methods can vary from state to state; Brad Jennings/Axios State of play: At the same time that Arizona's vaccination rates are dropping, the state is in the midst of its biggest measles outbreak in years. Arizona has seen 344 measles cases since the start of 2025, most of them concentrated in the vaccine-averse former polygamous Fundamentalist Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints enclave of Colorado City in the northern reaches of the state. Maricopa County has also seen 27 positive measles cases this year, the most in the county since at least 2006. Since 2025, 27 people in the state have been hospitalized due to measles. None have died. What they're saying: Vaccine advocates in the state found the exemption spike concerning. “My main takeaway is, this is really disappointing, but I’m not surprised,” Arizona Public Health Association executive director Will Humble told Arizona's Family. In a statement to Axios, Arizona Department of Health Services spokesperson Magda Rodríguez says the department works "with partners on strategies aimed at reducing barriers and increasing access, as well as building trust in vaccines." Between the lines: Polling shows childhood vaccines remain popular, but administration efforts to upend longstanding vaccine policies could further erode trust in vaccines and public health, medical experts say. President Trump last week signed an executive order calling for a slimmed-down childhood vaccine schedule and breaking the MMR vaccine into three separately administered shots. It also advises states to review federal vaccine recommendations and update their requirements for school enrollment. Yes, but: Vaccine scheduling recommendations or policy changes stemming from the order are expected to be challenged in court. Editor’s note: This story has been corrected to show 7,800 kids obtained an exemption from at least one vaccine last school year (not 74,000). B routine priorityPublic-health news

Several measles cases confirmed in Western New York; Steuben County health official weighs in on staying safe - WHEC.com

News Brief
Several measles cases have been confirmed in Western New York, including one in Steuben County, according to a report by News10NBC. Health officials note that the U.S. is experiencing its worst measles outbreak in 35 years, with at least two cases in Erie County, two in Allegany County, one in Seneca County, and one in Steuben County. The affected individuals are unvaccinated, and the health department is working to trace contacts and prevent further spread. The CDC reports that there are already more measles cases this year than in all of 2025, highlighting the severity of the outbreak. Unvaccinated individuals face a higher risk of severe complications, such as pneumonia, brain swelling, and potential hearing or vision loss. Health officials advise unvaccinated people to avoid crowds, practice good hygiene, and monitor for symptoms for 21 days after potential exposure. The Steuben and Seneca County cases are not yet reflected on the latest CDC map, indicating ongoing efforts to update and track the outbreak.

Advertisement Steuben County health official weighs in on staying safe from measles (live report at 6 a.m.) STEUBEN COUNTY, N.Y. — News10NBC is continuing to track measles cases in Western New York, amid what health officials call the U.S.’s worst measles outbreak in 35 years. There are at least two confirmed cases in Erie County, two in Allegany County, one in Seneca County, and now one in Steuben County. Kat Potter, the deputy health director in Steuben County, said the case is in an unvaccinated person, but did not say if the person was traveling. RELATED: Tracking measles cases in Western New York John Hopkins University shows that Steuben County has a 99.8% MMR vaccination rate. News10NBC’s Kristi Blake wanted to know how severe the cases are for those with and without their vaccination. “Measles in an unvaccinated person who’s been vaccinated with MMR, they have close to a 95 to 100% rate of never contracting the illness, when they’re exposed,” Potter said. “So that’s a great benefit and talks about how effective the vaccine is. If a vaccinated person were to contract measles, the symptoms would look much milder. You’d have a shorter illness and would lead to less severe complications in an unvaccinated person. As you can imagine, the symptoms potentially could be more severe and lead to complications such as pneumonia, brain swelling, dehydration, and potential hearing and vision loss.” Working to contain the disease After confirming measles, the public health department works quickly to contact anyone who had contact with the affected person during a specific time period. Similar to COVID-19 protocols, patients provide a list of who they’ve seen, where they’ve gone, and when, and the experts do the work from there. Centers for Disease Control data show there are already more measles cases this year nationally than in all of 2025. There are about 2,600 cases in the country. The Steuben and Seneca County cases are not yet reflected in the latest CDC map from last week. The CDC map shows a lot of cases in South Carolina, Virginia, and Pennsylvania, which borders New York. Blue dots on the map mean the infection was contracted locally. Red dots indicate someone who traveled. News10NBC asked Potter if health officials are worried about the continued spread. Potter said that’s a hard question. The health department would like to say no, but there’s always a possibility. For those traveling to a spot with known cases, Potter offered advice. “So that’s actually one of the things that you would look into before traveling somewhere. Look into making sure that you are up to date on your vaccines,” Potter said. “And if you choose not to be vaccinated or if you’re unsure of your status, you would be doing regular disease control and prevention. So you be trying to avoid crowds, making sure you’re washing your hands, taking care of yourself. You could potentially consider masking. And then you’d also want to monitor yourself for 21 days after you travel to an area where there was measles.” Symptoms of measles Measles is spread primarily through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes, or talks, releasing tiny droplets containing the virus. If you think you may have measles or you’ve been exposed, call your doctor. Do not go to a walk-in clinic or the emergency department. If you’re vaccinated, Potter said you have at least a 95% chance of never contracting measles. If you do, the symptoms will be mild. Think shortness of breath and rash versus brain swelling and potential vision and hearing loss. 美国 24 个州感染 麻疹 病例超过 900 例:CDC - ABC News - 突发新闻、最新新闻和视频

Measles cases surpass 900 in US with infections in 24 states: CDC - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos
新闻简报截至2026年2月13日,美国24个州报告了910例麻疹确诊病例,创下33年来的最高数字。CDC 将这些病例中的大多数归咎于未接种疫苗的个人或 疫苗接种 状态未知的人,其中很大一部分发生在南卡罗来纳州斯帕坦堡县,暴发 是去年开始的。麻疹 已蔓延到邻近县和其他州,包括佛罗里达州,万福玛利亚大学 (Ave Maria University) 经历了 麻疹 麻疹。只有 6 例与国际游客有关,3% 的患者住院。CDC 建议注射两剂 MMR 疫苗,有效率达 97%,但近年来 疫苗接种 的有效率有所下降。卫生官员强调,大多数病例是由于已知患者的密切接触造成的,而麻疹已经持续了一年,这引发了人们对麻疹消除状态可能丧失的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至 2026 年 2 月 13 日下午 3:32,南卡罗来纳州的 暴发 从去年开始并蔓延到其他州。CDC 数据显示,暴发 已持续一年,如 N17 和 N18 中所述。
地点与对象
暴发 已在 24 个州报告,包括亚利桑那州、加利福尼亚州、科罗拉多州、佛罗里达州、佐治亚州、爱达荷州、肯塔基州、缅因州、明尼苏达州、内布拉斯加州、纽约州、北卡罗来纳州、北达科他州、俄亥俄州、俄克拉荷马州、俄勒冈州、宾夕法尼亚州、南卡罗来纳州、南达科他州、犹他州、佛蒙特州、弗吉尼亚州、华盛顿州和威斯康星州。大多数病例是未接种疫苗的个体或 疫苗接种 状态未知的个体,如 N2 和 N6 中所述。
事件
根据CDC数据,美国麻疹病例已超过900例,24个州确诊910例感染。如 N11 和 N4 所示,这是 33 年来全国病例数量最多的一次。CDC 报告称,大多数病例是未接种疫苗或身份不明的个体,而南卡罗来纳州的 暴发 导致病例数量不断上升,如 N13 和 N6 中提到的。
规模、影响与风险
共有910例感染确诊,其中3%的患者住院治疗。CDC的数据显示,只有6例病例来自国际游客,大多数病例是19岁以下的人。暴发 已持续一年,感染蔓延到邻近县和其他州,如 N4、N10、N14 和 N18 中所述。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC 建议接种两剂 MMR 疫苗,一剂有效率 93%,两剂有效率 97%。然而,联邦数据显示 疫苗接种 率近年来一直滞后,如 N19 和 N20 中所述。CDC 正在监控 暴发,但尚未提供有关具体响应的进一步细节,如 N15 和 N16 中提到的。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases surpass 900 in US with infections in 24 states: CDC - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
As of February 13, 2026, the United States has reported 910 confirmed measles cases across 24 states, marking the highest number in 33 years. The CDC attributes the majority of these cases to unvaccinated individuals or those with unknown vaccination status, with a significant portion occurring in Spartanburg County, South Carolina, where the outbreak began last year. The outbreak has spread to neighboring counties and other states, including Florida, where Ave Maria University experienced a measles outbreak. Only six cases were linked to international visitors, and 3% of patients were hospitalized. The CDC recommends two doses of the MMR vaccine, which are 97% effective, but vaccination rates have declined in recent years. Health officials emphasize that most cases are due to close contacts of known patients, and the outbreak has been ongoing for a year, raising concerns about the potential loss of measles elimination status.

OffOn Stream on Measles cases surpass 900 in US with infections in 24 states: CDC The majority of cases are among the unvaccinated or those with unknown status. ByMary Kekatos February 13, 2026, 3:32 PM At least 177 new measles cases have been reported in the last week in the U.S., according to newly updated data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. A total of 910 infections have been confirmed in 24 states including Arizona, California, Colorado, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Kentucky, Maine, Minnesota, Nebraska, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, South Carolina, South Dakota, Utah, Vermont, Virginia, Washington and Wisconsin. Only six measles cases this year were from international visitors, CDC data shows. Related New map shows how to spot the measles risk level in your ZIP code About 94% of cases are among people who are unvaccinated or whose vaccination status is unknown, the CDC said. Meanwhile, 2% of cases are among those who have received just one dose of the measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine and 3% of cases are among those who received the recommended two doses, according to the CDC. CDC data shows that the majority of cases occur among patients under 19. About 3% of all measles patients in the U.S. have been hospitalized. Last year, the U.S. recorded 2,280 measles cases, which is the highest number of national cases seen in 33 years, according to the CDC. In this Feb. 6, 2026, file photo, a sign is shown outside a mobile clinic offering measles and flu vaccinations in Spartanburg, South Carolina. Sean Rayford/Getty Images, FILE A measles outbreak in South Carolina, which began last year, has contributed to the rising number of national cases. Health officials say the majority of cases are close contacts of known patients. Additionally, at Ave Maria University in southern Florida, a measles outbreak has led to dozens of cases, according to health and campus officials. Last month marked one year since a measles outbreak began in West Texas, with infections soon spreading to neighboring counties and other states. Related Mexico could be at risk of losing measles elimination status after more than 9,000 cases since last year Public health experts previously told ABC News that if cases in other states are found to be linked to the cases in Texas, it would mean the virus has been spreading for a year, which could lead to a loss of elimination status. The CDC currently recommends that people receive two doses of the MMR vaccine, the first at ages 12 to 15 months and the second between 4 and 6 years old. One dose is 93% effective, and two doses are 97% effective against measles, the CDC says. However, federal data shows vaccination rates have been lagging in recent years. During the 2024-2025 school year, 92.5% of kindergartners received the MMR vaccine, according to data. This is lower than the 92.7% seen in the previous school year and the 95.2% seen in the 2019-2020 school year, before the COVID-19 pandemic. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles case reported at Pittsburgh hospital, marking Allegheny County's first since 2019 - CBS News

News Brief
A measles case was confirmed at UPMC Children's Hospital in Pittsburgh, marking the first in Allegheny County since 2019. The patient, who was unvaccinated, was at the hospital's emergency department on August 20, 2026. UPMC notified all individuals who may have been exposed, and the Allegheny County Health Department stated there is no ongoing risk of measles transmission at the hospital. The case highlights a broader trend of measles outbreaks in the U.S., with Pennsylvania reporting 340 cases this year. Officials emphasized that measles is highly contagious but vaccine-preventable, with one dose of the measles-mumps-rubella vaccine being 93% effective and two doses 97% effective. The patient's origin was not disclosed due to privacy reasons, but the health department urged vaccination to prevent further spread.

A spokesperson for UPMC said in a statement on Thursday that everyone who may have been exposed to the patient, who was unvaccinated, has been notified. There's no ongoing risk of contracting measles at Children's Hospital, UPMC said. The Allegheny County Health Department said the patient was at the emergency department on Aug. 12 and Aug. 18. The patient isn't from Allegheny County, but officials didn't release any more information, citing privacy reasons. This case in Pittsburgh comes as outbreaks spread in multiple states. So far this year, the U.S. has recorded the most measles cases in more than three decades. Ongoing outbreaks have put the country at risk of losing its official measles elimination status, which it has had since 2000. The Pennsylvania Department of Health says that, as of Wednesday, the state has reported 340 cases this year, with about half in Lancaster County. It's Allegheny County's first reported case of measles in 2019. The Allegheny County Health Department says measles is a highly contagious disease — up to 90% of people exposed to measles who are not immune will become infected. But the disease is vaccine-preventable. One dose of the measles-mumps-rubella vaccine is 93% effective against measles and two doses are 97% effective, the county health department says. The county health department recommends that people get vaccinated against measles to protect themselves and prevent the spread of the disease. UPMC says if you think you or your child has been exposed to someone with measles or has symptoms consistent with measles, which include a fever and a rash, alert your healthcare provider before arriving at a facility. The health system also noted that vaccination is "highly effective" at preventing measles. 梅格斯县有四例确诊的 麻疹 病例 - Athens County Independent

Four confirmed measles cases in Meigs County - Athens County Independent

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Four confirmed measles cases in Meigs County - Athens County Independent

News Brief
Four confirmed measles cases were reported in Meigs County, Ohio, as of August 17, 2026, by the Meigs County Health Department. These cases are part of a broader outbreak in northeast and central Ohio, where the Ohio Department of Health has recorded 29 cases this month. The measles virus is highly infectious and can survive in the air for up to two hours. Symptoms typically appear seven to 14 days after exposure, though it can take up to 21 days. The Meigs County Health Department is collaborating with the Ohio Department of Health to track potential exposures. Residents are advised to contact their primary care provider if they suspect exposure, and the Athens City-County Health Department offers vaccination services to those without insurance or with poor coverage. Vaccination is emphasized as the primary method of prevention, with the MMR vaccine being 97% effective in preventing the disease for life. The outbreak highlights the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates, which have declined in recent years due to increased exemptions for school entry.

Four confirmed measles cases in Meigs County By Eric Boll August 20, 2026 Features, Health & Safety MEIGS COUNTY, Ohio — As of Aug. 17, four cases of measles have been confirmed by the Meigs County Health Department, which is now working with the Ohio Department of Health to track potential exposures. A press release from the organization notes that measles is highly infectious. An infected person can infect others, even if they aren’t showing symptoms. The virus can survive in air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves a room. Symptoms of measles include high fever, cough, runny nose, watery eyes, and a rash which begins three to five days after other symptoms. Generally, measles symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, although it can take up to 21 days for symptoms to occur. About one in five people who catch the measles virus will be hospitalized with severe symptoms including pneumonia, severe dehydration and brain swelling, among others. If you think you have been exposed to measles, you should contact your primary care provider for guidance, Jack Pepper, administrator of the Athens City-County Health Department, told Athens County Independent. “If you suspect that you may have been exposed or have contracted the disease, you should put your provider on notice when you arrive that you think [you’ve been exposed to measles], because they’re likely to isolate you just because it is such a contagious disease,” Pepper said. Concerned residents may opt to wear personal protective equipment if they are worried about exposure, Pepper said. The Ohio Department of Health has recorded 29 cases of measles this month in northeast and central Ohio. The Meigs County cases had not been recorded at publication time. No other counties in Southeast Ohio had reported measles cases as of Aug. 20. Vaccination prevents measles In July Bruce Vanderhoff, the director of the Ohio Department of Health, encouraged the public to get the MMR vaccine in a press release following Ohio’s third measles outbreak this year. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Vanderhoff said. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Measles poses additional risks for pregnant women, whose babies may be delivered prematurely or with low birth weight. One of the primary ways to protect oneself against measles is to get vaccinated. The MMR (measles, mumps and rubella) vaccine is usually given in two doses to children and is 97% effective in keeping a person illness free for life. “That’s our number one recommendation, is to get the vaccine,” Pepper said. Those looking to get the vaccine should contact their primary care provider or local health department. Pepper said the Athens City-County Health Department will administer the vaccine to those without insurance or with poor insurance. “We do not turn people away, so you can call us anytime, and we will get you taken care of,” Pepper said. About 3% of people vaccinated still contract measles, mumps or rubella, but their symptoms are usually mild, according to Yale Medicine. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control recommends that adults receive at least one booster dose of the MMR vaccine. Some adults should receive a second dose, including: Students at post-high school educational institutions Healthcare personnel International travelers Adults who are household contacts or other close contacts of people who are immunocompromised Adults living with HIV who are not severely immunocompromised Groups at increased risk because of an outbreak Measles infections declined steadily following the introduction of a measles vaccine in 1963. Up until about 2019, the vaccination rate among U.S. kindergarteners was above 95% — the threshold for herd immunity. Increasing numbers of states added exemptions from vaccination for school entry, however, and vaccination rates began to fall. Vaccination rates have continued to fall, dropping to 92% for the MMR vaccine for the 2025–26 school year. The decline in vaccination rates has coincided with a resurgence in measles outbreaks. Athens City-County Health Department, Meigs County Eric Boll Eric Boll is a Ph.D. student in the E.W. Scripps School of Journalism specializing in science communication and science news. He works for the Athens County Independent through a grant program organized by the Sugarbush Foundation. When not working Eric can be found cooking, playing with his cats, riding his bike or working on a stained glass project. Notifications

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles case confirmed in Orange County; exposure warnings issued at multiple restaurants, public spaces - CBS News

News Brief
On August 19, 2026, Orange County officials confirmed a measles case involving an adult who visited several public spaces, including the Anaheim Packing District, Fire and Ice restaurant, BJ's restaurant, The Escape Game Orange, and UCI Health Fountain Valley Cafeteria. The individual was infected from a local exposure, and health authorities issued warnings to those who may have been in contact. The Orange County Health Care Agency advised people to check their immunity status and monitor for symptoms such as high fever, cough, runny nose, red and watery eyes, and a rash appearing 3-5 days after other signs. Those not fully immunized or unsure of their immunity were urged to consult healthcare providers about the MMR vaccine. Nationally, there have been 2,566 confirmed measles cases in 2026, the highest since the disease was declared eliminated in the U.S. Health officials emphasized that anyone without symptoms after 21 days is no longer at risk. The agency also highlighted the importance of vaccination as the best protection against measles, urging clinicians to report suspected cases immediately for guidance and testing coordination.

According to the Orange County Health Care Agency, the person was infected from a local exposure. "Measles is highly contagious. Everyone 12 months and older should receive two doses of MMR vaccine (measles, mumps and rubella). Infants less than a year old who are traveling internationally should receive one dose of MMR vaccine. Adults and others who are not immune remain at risk, especially as outbreaks continue across the country and worldwide," said Dr. Anissa Davis, Deputy County Health Officer. "Vaccination is the best protection." During their infectious period, the person visited the following places: Aug. 11 – 5 p.m. to 10 p.m. – Anaheim Packing District Aug. 14 – 5 p.m. to 8 p.m. – Fire and Ice restaurant, Anaheim Aug. 14 – 10 p.m. - 12 midnight – BJ's restaurant, Orange Aug. 14 – 8 p.m. - 11 p.m. – The Escape Game Orange Aug. 11 and Aug. 14 – 10:40 a.m. to 12 p.m. – UCI Health Fountain Valley Cafeteria OC HCA instructed people in those areas within those timeframes to do the following: Review whether they are immune to measles or not. People who are not fully immunized against measles or who don't know if they are immune to measles already, should talk with a health care provider about receiving the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine. Monitor themselves for illness with fever and/or an unexplained rash from 7 days to 21 days after their exposure (the time period when symptoms may develop); if symptoms develop, stay at home and call a health care provider immediately for guidance. Anyone who suspects they may have measles should call their medical provider before arriving at the medical office to avoid exposing others. Clinicians should immediately report any suspected measles cases to the OC Health Care Agency for guidance and testing coordination. Anyone who does not develop symptoms after 21 days is no longer considered at risk of contracting measles, according to health officials. B routine priorityPublic-health news

As measles outbreaks grow in the U.S., maps and charts show a record number of cases - CBS News

News Brief
In 2026, the U.S. has seen a surge in measles outbreaks, with a record number of confirmed cases surpassing those of 2025, which was the worst year in recent history. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) data highlights that measles cases have increased significantly, particularly in communities with lower vaccination rates. This trend has raised concerns about the U.S. potentially losing its measles elimination status since 2000. The CBS News data team has been tracking these cases as new information is released by state health departments and the CDC. Health experts point to declining vaccination rates as a key factor, with the CDC emphasizing that maintaining over 95% coverage is essential for community immunity. Dr. Jay Bhattacharya, director of the National Institutes of Health, reiterated the importance of vaccination and noted no evidence linking it to increased autism rates. Vaccines are also subject to extensive safety testing, ensuring their effectiveness and reliability.

Read Full Bio Grace Manthey Updated on: August 19, 2026 / 9:51 AM EDT / CBS News Add CBS News on Google So far in 2026, the U.S. has recorded the most measles cases in more than three decades as outbreaks spread in multiple states, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention data shows. By mid-July, the number had already surpassed all of 2025, which had been the worst year in recent history with more than 2,200 confirmed cases. Ongoing outbreaks have put the U.S. at risk of losing its official measles elimination status, which it has held since 2000. Measles is one of the most contagious infectious diseases. In some cases, it can cause severe infections in the lungs and brain that can lead to cognitive issues, deafness or death. But it can be prevented by vaccination, which medical research has proven to be safe and highly effective. The CBS News data team has tracked confirmed measles cases nationwide as new data is released by state health departments and the CDC. Out of the 2,289 confirmed cases last year, more than 750 were connected to an outbreak in West Texas that led to the deaths of two children. Prior to 2025, the highest number of confirmed cases in the U.S. in recent years was 1,274 in 2019, driven by outbreaks in New York, California and Washington state. Most years the total was much lower. Health experts point to lower vaccination rates as the main reason for increases in preventable infectious diseases like measles. The vast majority of cases have been in people who were unvaccinated, and outbreaks have spread in communities with lower vaccination rates. CDC data shows about 92.4% of kindergartners in the U.S. got their measles-mumps-rubella shots in the 2025-2026 school year, down slightly from 92.5% the previous year. The figure has declined from 95.2% during the 2019-2020 school year — a critical threshold to keep people safe. Only 10 states hit the 95% threshold last year. "When more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated, most people are protected through community immunity (herd immunity)," the CDC states. "I think that parents, for most kids, especially for the measles vaccines, the DTP, all the standard childhood vaccines, it's very important that parents vaccinate their kids," Dr. Jay Bhattacharya, the director of the National Institutes of Health, said on "Face the Nation with Margaret Brennan." Research has found no evidence of increased rates of autism among those who are vaccinated compared to those who are not. Vaccines also undergo intensive safety testing. Measles outbreak in southwestern Wisconsin grows, 2 cases confirmed in Grant County

威斯康星州卫生服务部 (DHS) 确认了 麻疹 麻疹,同一天格兰特县报告了另外两例病例;病例与邻近县(N5、N6)的先前病例有关。该事件已确认并正在进行中。

截至报告日期 (N7),威斯康星州西南部有 36 例确诊的 麻疹 病例和 3 例可能病例。所提供的证据中没有死亡报告。未确定公共暴露地点 (N8)。根据报告中引用的 CDC 数据 (N3),截至 12 月 30 日,2026 年美国 麻疹 病例总数为 2,065 例。
应对、状态与不确定性
每个县的公共卫生官员正在识别并通知可能暴露的个人 (N9)。卫生当局建议居民识别 麻疹 症状,如果出现症状,即使没有已知的暴露情况,也应联系医疗保健提供者 (N10)。麻疹 可以通过 MMR 疫苗 (N13) 来预防。提供的证据(N9、N10、N13)中没有提供具体的隔离、接触者追踪详细信息或 治疗 更新。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles outbreak in southwestern Wisconsin grows, 2 cases confirmed in Grant County

News Brief
Southwestern Wisconsin’s measles outbreak expanded on August 18, 2026, after the Wisconsin Department of Health Services confirmed two new cases in Grant County, bringing the regional total to 36 confirmed and three probable cases. The new cases are linked to previously confirmed infections in adjacent Lafayette and Iowa Counties. No public exposure sites have been disclosed, and public health teams are actively tracing contacts. Authorities urge residents to watch for measles symptoms—including high fever, cough, conjunctivitis, and a characteristic rash—and to call a healthcare provider before visiting clinics to prevent further spread. Measles remains preventable through vaccination with the MMR vaccine.

Natalya Maisheva/iStockphoto/Getty Images via CNN Newsource Facebook Twitter WhatsApp SMS Email Print Copy article link Save MADISON, Wis. -- The measles outbreak in southwestern Wisconsin continues to grow, the Wisconsin Department of Health Services said, after two more cases were confirmed Tuesday in Grant County. The DHS said the cases are related to the cases confirmed in Lafayette and Iowa Counties last week. There are now 36 confirmed measles cases in southwestern Wisconsin and three probable cases. No public exposure locations have been identified in connection with the cases. The DHS said public health officials in each county are working to identify and notify those who may have been exposed to the virus. Residents should know the symptoms of measles and call a healthcare provider if they develop symptoms, even if they are not aware of a specific exposure. Symptoms of measles typically appear approximately 10 to 21 days after being exposed to the virus, and include: Runny nose High fever (may be greater than 104°F) Tiredness Cough Red, watery eyes, or conjunctivitis (pink eye) A red rash with raised bumps that starts at the hairline and moves to the arms and legs three to five days after symptoms begin. Anyone developing symptoms of measles should stay home (not go to work, school, social gatherings, shopping, or use public transportation) and call a doctor’s office or clinic before visiting so they can take precautions to ensure other patients are not exposed to the virus. Measles can be prevented with the measles, mumps, rubella vaccine (MMR). COPYRIGHT 2026 BY CHANNEL 3000. ALL RIGHTS RESERVED. Four measles cases reported in Meigs County - Athens County Independent

News Brief
The Meigs County Health Department in Ohio reported four confirmed measles cases on August 18, 2026. The Ohio Department of Health is collaborating with local authorities to trace contacts and promote vaccination opportunities. Measles is highly contagious and can lead to severe complications, including hospitalization in one-fifth of cases. Health officials urge individuals with symptoms to contact their providers before visiting to prevent further spread. Vaccination status should be verified with healthcare providers, as post-exposure interventions may be available.

Contact Volunteer Four measles cases reported in Meigs County By Meigs County Health Department August 18, 2026 Announcements MEIGS COUNTY, Ohio – The Meigs County Health Department (MCHD) is reporting four measles cases in Meigs County, Ohio. The Ohio Department of Health (ODH) is working with MCHD to follow up on potential exposures and promote opportunities for vaccination. MCHD is working with the individuals to identify contacts. The contacts will be notified by their local health department and provided guidance on quarantining. Symptoms of measles can include high fever, cough, runny nose, watery eyes, and a rash beginning three to five (3-5) days after other symptoms occur. Start of symptoms may be delayed in people with compromised immune systems. Measles can be serious, and about one out of every five people (20%) who get measles will be hospitalized with complications like pneumonia, dehydration, or swelling of the brain. In pregnant women who have not had the MMR vaccine, measles may cause a premature birth, or a low-birth-weight baby. Measles is a very contagious virus that can spread easily to others, even before a person has symptoms. The measles virus can live for up to two (2) hours in the air after an infected person leaves the room. Symptoms usually appear 7 – 14 days after a person comes in contact with the virus, but it can take as long as 21 days for symptoms to appear. Please note: Public Health does not provide testing or treatment for measles. If you are experiencing symptoms, avoid contact with others and seek care from your healthcare provider. Call the provider before you arrive to notify them you have a measles concern, so that further spread can be prevented. Although the likelihood of getting measles is low in vaccinated individuals, it is still important to stay alert and take precautions. It is important to note that individuals born before 1957 likely have immunity to measles through exposure. The measles (MMR) vaccine is recommended at 12 – 15 months and again at 4 – 6 years of age. If you are unsure of your vaccination status, contact your healthcare provider. Immunizations or medications may be given after exposure in some cases to prevent illness, again, contact your healthcare provider for additional information. More information and updates will be shared through a media release and the MCHD social media page when available. For more information about measles, visit the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention website. Meigs County Health Department Notifications

Four cases reported; measles is highly contagious with potential for severe complications including hospitalization (20% of cases), pneumonia, dehydration, brain swelling, and adverse outcomes in pregnant women. Transmission can occur up to two hours after an infected person leaves a room.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Ohio Department of Health (ODH) is collaborating with MCHD to trace contacts and promote vaccination. Contacts will be notified and provided quarantine guidance. Public health officials advise seeking care from healthcare providers if symptoms appear, calling ahead to prevent further spread. No testing or treatment is provided by public health; vaccination status should be verified with healthcare providers.

Steuben County reported a second measles case, with a total of four unvaccinated residents testing positive for the virus. This is confirmed by the evidence.
Scale, impact and risk
Four unvaccinated residents have tested positive for measles. The outbreak has spread to multiple counties, indicating a potential risk of further transmission among unvaccinated individuals.
Response, status and uncertainty
The outbreak is under investigation, and the response includes monitoring and reporting of cases. The source mentions the need for public health measures, but no specific actions are detailed.

兰开斯特县两周内麻疹病例激增,跃居美国第2位

Lancaster County's 2-week spike in measles cases moves it into #2 ranking in US
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县在连续两周激增 63 例后,已成为 2026 年美国 麻疹 病例数第二高的县,使总数达到 162 例。宾夕法尼亚州全州范围内记录了 307 例病例,其中 52 例住院,没有死亡,而美国疾病控制和预防中心报告全国有 2,566 例病例,超过了本世纪的年度总数。卫生当局将 暴发 归因于 疫苗接种 覆盖率低,并指出所有确诊病例都涉及未接受完整两剂 MMR 计划的个人。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部正在受影响地区部署临时 疫苗接种 诊所,自 4 月底 暴发 开始以来已注射了 1,800 多剂疫苗。泛美卫生组织将于11月决定是否撤销美国的麻疹消除地位。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-17;事件期间包括在发布之前结束的为期 2 周的峰值以及截至 2026 年报告的累积病例。
地点与对象
美国宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县;受影响人群包括确诊感染 麻疹 的居民。
事件
兰开斯特县的 麻疹 病例在两周内增加了 63 例,使其成为 2026 年美国第二高的县级总数;该排名由约翰霍普金斯大学彭博公共卫生学院 麻疹 追踪器发布。
规模、影响与风险
截至本报告发布,兰卡斯特县在 2026 年报告了 162 起 麻疹 病例;宾夕法尼亚州全州报告了 307 例病例,其中 52 例住院治疗(17%),没有报告死亡病例;美国疾病预防控制中心 (CDC) 报告,2026 年有 2,566 例 麻疹 病例,超过本世纪每年的总数;麻疹 严重时可能致命。
应对、状态与不确定性
宾夕法尼亚州卫生部表示,所有确诊的 麻疹 病例都涉及未完全接种疫苗的个人(少于两剂 MMR);该部门正在受影响地区组织临时 疫苗 诊所,自 4 月下旬以来已管理了 1,800 多个 疫苗;泛美卫生组织可能于 2026 年 11 月审查美国 麻疹 消除状况。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Lancaster County's 2-week spike in measles cases moves it into #2 ranking in US

News Brief
Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, has become the U.S. county with the second-highest number of measles cases in 2026 after a two-week surge added 63 cases, bringing its total to 162. Statewide, Pennsylvania has recorded 307 cases, including 52 hospitalizations and no deaths, while the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reports 2,566 cases nationwide—exceeding annual totals this century. Health authorities attribute the outbreak to low vaccination coverage, noting all confirmed cases involved individuals who had not received the full two-dose MMR schedule. The Pennsylvania Department of Health is deploying pop-up vaccination clinics in affected areas and has administered more than 1,800 doses since the outbreak began in late April. The Pan American Health Organization will decide in November whether to revoke the U.S.’s measles elimination status.
Original source text (English)

SUZETTE WENGER | Staff Photographer Facebook Twitter Email Print Copy article link Lancaster County now ranks second in the United States for number of measles cases in a county or region this year, after a 63-case rise in the number of people sickened with the virus in the past two weeks. The ranking was published by the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health measles tracker on Friday. As of today, the county’s total has increased to 162 cases so far this year. Statewide, 307 people have been sickened with the virus in 2026. On its measles dashboard, Johns Hopkins listed one fewer case for Lancaster County -- reporting a 62-case rise in the past two weeks. Although there haven’t been any deaths associated with measles reported in Pennsylvania this year, at its worst, the virus can be fatal. Of Pennsylvania residents who have fallen ill with the virus, 52 people, or 17%, have been hospitalized. “All individuals with a confirmed measles infection in Pennsylvania are not fully vaccinated,” said Neil Ruhland, press secretary for the Pennsylvania Department of Health “To be considered fully vaccinated against measles, someone needs to have received two doses of the MMR vaccine.” Nonprofit local news isn't free! It takes reporters, editors, and resources to do this work right. Your donation keeps that work alive. MAKE YOUR DONATION TODAY The measles vaccine is safe and is 97% effective when a person receives two doses. The Department of Health continues to organize pop-up vaccine clinics in areas where the virus has been identified. Since the current measles outbreak started in late April, state healthcare workers have administered over 1,800 vaccines in these areas, Ruhland said. Once a disease that was considered eliminated in the U.S., the virus has made a raging comeback. The federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention has reported 2,566 measles cases in 2026, a number higher than the total cases for each year this century. The Pan American Health Organization, the entity which grants the elimination status, meets in November. It will decide then if the United States’ measles elimination status is revoked.
